### [92] [Re-Centering Humans in LLM Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06614)
*Lechen Zhang,Jiarui Liu,Tal August*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Synthetic benchmarks overestimate LLM personalization quality. With real human conversations and judgments, current systems fail at extracting, selecting, and using personal information reliably; small training fixes help earlier stages, but judging truly useful personalization still does not align well with humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior evaluations of LLM personalization mostly use synthetic data, so it is unclear whether reported performance reflects how well these systems work for real users. The paper aims to measure the gap between synthetic-data evaluation and human-data evaluation.

Method: The paper compares LLM personalization performance on synthetic versus human data across three stages: user attribute extraction, relevant attribute selection for new prompts, and personalized response generation. It builds a human dataset of 550 conversations and human judgments for each stage, then tests systems and two lightweight training-based interventions.

Result: On human data, models perform worse at extracting attributes, disagree with humans on which attributes are relevant, and produce personalized responses that humans do not rate better than generic ones, despite LLM judges often preferring them. The proposed lightweight training interventions improve alignment with human data in the first two stages, while reward models in the final stage show only modest correlation with human ratings.

Conclusion: Using real human data exposes substantial weaknesses in current LLM personalization pipelines; lightweight training helps early-stage evaluation, but modeling human-aligned judgments of final personalized response quality remains difficult.

Abstract: Despite growing interest, most evaluations of large language models' (LLMs') personalization abilities have relied on synthetic data. It remains unclear how well current personalization systems work for real users. In this paper, we study the gap in LLM personalization performance when using synthetic versus human data. We collect human conversations (550 conversations) and judgments across three stages of personalization: extracting user attributes from conversations (5,949 judgments), pairing relevant attributes with new prompts (11,919), and incorporating relevant attributes into a personalized response (1,101). Incorporating human data reveals system limitations at each stage. Models struggle to extract attributes from human conversations, disagree with human judgments on relevant attributes, and generate personalized responses that humans judge no better than generic responses (though that LLM judges widely rate as better). We introduce two lightweight training-based interventions that shift automated personalization evaluation closer to human data in our first two stages. However, in our third stage we find that learned reward models achieve only modest correlation with human ratings, suggesting that human-aligned personalization quality judgments are difficult to model directly. Our collected data provides a foundation for studying how models should extract, select, and incorporate user information in ways that humans find useful.

</details>


### [93] [UnpredictaBench: A Benchmark for Evaluating Distributional Randomness in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06622)
*Amirhossein Abaskohi,Amirhossein Dabiriaghdam,Liang Luo,Ellie Dingqiao Wen,Lele Wang,Giuseppe Carenini,Peter West*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper builds a benchmark to test whether LLMs can sample from the right probability distributions rather than just produce varied answers, and finds that current models perform poorly, leaving major room for improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as substitutes for humans or other entities in simulations, but many models collapse to a narrow set of plausible answers instead of reflecting the true uncertainty and variability of real systems. Existing diversity-focused methods are not enough because simulation requires calibrated sampling from target distributions, not just diverse outputs.

Method: The paper introduces UnpredictaBench, a benchmark of 448 distribution-sampling problems spanning canonical statistical distributions, stochastic-program-induced distributions, and natural-language descriptions of random processes. It also proposes KS@N, a metric based on the Kolmogorov-Smirnov test that measures how often model-generated samples of size N are statistically indistinguishable from ground-truth samples.

Result: Across both open and proprietary models, the benchmark finds a wide range of distributional performance. At KS@100, scores range from nearly 0 to above 20%, but no model exceeds 40%. Reasoning can help somewhat, yet there is no clear immediate fix for the core problem.

Conclusion: UnpredictaBench reveals that even simplified distributional simulation is still difficult for current LLMs; no tested model reliably matches target distributions at strong sample sizes, so substantial capability gaps remain before LLMs can serve as faithful stand-ins for complex stochastic systems.

Abstract: We introduce UnpredictaBench, an evaluation that tests the ability of large language models (LLMs) to capture true underlying distributions. As LLMs are increasingly used as substitutes for other entities (e.g., for humans in economic simulations), the tendency of many models to collapse towards a single plausible answer means a failure to capture the unpredictability of real systems. Recent work on improving output diversity is insufficient for this setting: simulation requires samples that are calibrated to a target distribution, not merely varied outputs. UnpredictaBench isolates a simplified but fundamental version of this problem: sampling outcomes from individual target distributions, including canonical statistical distributions, distributions induced by stochastic programs, and natural-language scenarios that describe random processes. We introduce 448 such problems together with KS@N, a general-purpose evaluation metric that quantifies how well a model outputs approximate black-box target distributions via the Kolmogorov-Smirnov statistical test. This is the rate at which we fail to reject model samples of size N against ground-truth samples, with larger N indicating greater difficulty. Tested across open and proprietary models, we find a large spread in distributional capabilities. For instance, when models generate samples of size 100 (KS@100, our standard metric), scores range from near 0 to over 20%. No model is able to achieve over 40% at KS@100, showing significant headroom in distributional sampling as a capability. Although adding reasoning can somewhat increase scores, we find no immediate solution for this issue. UnpredictaBench shows that even simple distributional simulation remains challenging, making it a necessary first step toward using LLMs as stand-ins for complex systems.

</details>


### [94] [How Language Models Fail: Token-Level Signatures of Committed and Persistent Reasoning Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06635)
*Tanvi Thoria,Kiana Jafari,Marc R. Schlichting,Mykel J. Kochenderfer*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLM reasoning errors tend to happen in two ways: either the model commits early to a wrong path, or it stays uncertain throughout. Token-level uncertainty can distinguish these modes and helps decide the best failure-detection strategy, including when self-consistency is useful.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by the need to understand not just whether language model reasoning fails, but how those failures unfold over time in the reasoning trace so that detection methods can be better targeted.

Method: The paper analyzes token-level uncertainty signals along reasoning traces and compares successful vs. failing completions. It defines diagnostic signatures for two failure processes, tests falsifiable predictions across 23 model-dataset settings, and studies how these signatures interact with self-consistency.

Result: The proposed framework is empirically supported in 20 of 23 model-dataset configurations, significantly above chance for both failure modes. It also shows practical value by indicating when uncertainty-based detection adds to self-consistency and when self-consistency can be skipped.

Conclusion: LLM reasoning failures can be separated into two detectable modes: committed failure and persistent uncertainty. These modes leave distinct token-level uncertainty signatures that indicate when failures become detectable and suggest different detection strategies.

Abstract: Failures in language model reasoning emerge through distinct processes that leave identifiable signatures in the reasoning trace. We characterize these failures using token-level uncertainty signals, finding they arise through two empirically distinguishable processes. The first is committed failure, in which a model locks onto an incorrect reasoning path early in its trace. A central diagnostic signature is the commitment point, beyond which considering additional tokens hurt rather than help failure detection. In the second, persistent uncertainty, uncertainty instead accumulates throughout, and the full trace is needed to best distinguish failing from successful completions. These signatures reproduce across 23 model-dataset configurations, with the framework's falsifiable predictions holding in 20 of 23 cases, well above chance across both failure modes. Finally, we demonstrate our failure mode framework has direct implications for self-consistency, identifying when uncertainty signals complement it and when it can be selectively skipped. These results offer a foundation for understanding when LLM reasoning failures become detectable and for adapting detection strategies accordingly.

</details>


### [95] [CAF-Gen: A Multi-Agent System for Enriching Argumentation Structures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06646)
*Jakub Bąba,Jarosław Chudziak*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CAF-Gen uses a creator-reviewer multi-agent loop to convert shallow argument mining outputs into richer CAF argument models, improving structure quality and consistency over single-pass generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that existing argument mining systems capture only basic claims and premises and fail to model the richer formal structures required by advanced frameworks like CAF, including premise types, proof standards, and argument schemes.

Method: The method introduces CAF-Gen, a multi-agent Creator-Reviewer pipeline in which a creator agent generates CAF-compliant argument structures from shallow argument representations and a reviewer agent iteratively critiques and refines them to ensure structural integrity.

Result: Experiments show that the iterative feedback loop improves output quality, produces structurally richer argument models, and achieves strong agreement with original annotations compared with single-pass generative approaches.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that CAF-Gen, an iterative multi-agent framework, can reliably transform shallow argument mining outputs into structurally richer Carneades Argumentation Framework models, outperforming single-pass generation in structural stability and annotation alignment.

Abstract: Formalizing complex reasoning from natural text is one of the central challenges in computational linguistics. It requires systems to understand not just keywords but also the context and complex reasoning embedded in a text. Current Argument Mining (AM) techniques identify basic claims and premises, yet they often struggle to capture the richer structural information required by advanced schemas such as the Carneades Argumentation Framework (CAF), which incorporates features such as premise types, proof standards, and argument schemes. We address this limitation by introducing CAF-Gen, an automated multi-agent framework designed to enrich shallow argument structures into CAF-compliant argument models. By employing an iterative Creator-Reviewer pipeline, a creator agent's output is validated by a critical agent to ensure structural integrity. This multi-agent collaboration is crucial for mitigating the structural instability typical of single-pass generative models. Our experiments demonstrate that the iterative feedback loop improves the quality of the resulting data and achieves strong alignment with the original annotations, while producing structurally richer models. Our findings show that the multi-agent system can overcome the limitations of single-pass generation, providing a robust methodology for the automated modeling of formal argumentation.

</details>


### [96] [The Piggyback Hypothesis of Generalization: Explaining and Mitigating Emergent Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06667)
*Jiachen Zhao,Zhengxuan Wu,Aryaman Arora,Yiyou Sun,David Bau,Weiyan Shi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Chat-template prefix tokens can unintentionally carry finetuned behavior into unrelated queries; regularizing those tokens during finetuning (TReFT) reduces emergent misalignment and preserves task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by the unclear mechanism behind broad over-generalization in LLM finetuning, especially emergent misalignment where training on narrow tasks unexpectedly causes broad misalignment on semantically unrelated queries.

Method: The authors propose the Piggyback Hypothesis, test it by perturbing or representation-patching prefix tokens, and introduce Token-Regularized Finetuning (TReFT), which regularizes selected token representations during training to prevent the prefix from carrying misaligned behavior across domains.

Result: Experiments across multiple models and emergent-misalignment-inducing datasets show that prefix perturbation or patching can restore alignment, supporting the Piggyback Hypothesis. TReFT reduces emergent misalignment while preserving in-domain performance, outperforming data interleaving on Llama-3.1-8B in the legal domain by 33.5% in EM reduction, and reducing off-topic generalization by 54.3% on average in other narrow finetuning settings.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that emergent misalignment in narrowly finetuned LLMs can be driven by shared chat-template prefix tokens that transfer finetuned behavior to unrelated domains, and that constraining these token representations during finetuning can substantially reduce this effect without hurting task learning.

Abstract: The mechanisms behind LLMs' broad over-generalization beyond training examples remain unclear. Emergent misalignment (EM) offers a striking case study: finetuning on narrow tasks induces broad misalignment to semantically-unrelated test domains. In this work, we propose the Piggyback Hypothesis: the chat-template tokens can piggyback the finetuned behaviour onto out-of-domain queries. We validate this hypothesis by showing that subtle perturbations to the prefix (tokens preceding all user queries), or patching the prefix representations with those from the unfinetuned model, can restore alignment without changing the user query. Building on this finding, we propose Token-Regularized Finetuning (TReFT), which regularizes specific token representations during training to mitigate EM. Across different models and multiple EM-inducing datasets, TReFT reduces EM while preserving in-domain learning. On Llama-3.1-8B finetuned on the legal domain, TReFT achieves 33.5% more EM reduction than data interleaving with a retain set of aligned examples. We further show that TReFT extends to other narrow-finetuning settings, including abstention, tool use, and refusal (off-topic generalization is reduced by 54.3% on average), supporting the Piggyback Hypothesis. Broadly, our work highlights that LLMs may learn and generalize in unintended ways and suggests a path toward more constrained finetuning. It also calls for further study of how shared input features can piggyback model behavior across domains.

</details>


### [97] [What Do People Actually Want From AI? Mapping Preference Plurality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06674)
*Julia Sepúlveda Coelho,Scott A. Hale*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: People do not want one single thing from AI. Even shared goals like truthfulness mean different things to different users, so current RLHF alignment methods likely oversimplify and misrepresent human preferences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study is motivated by known limitations of RLHF, including aggregation of conflicting preferences, unrepresentative sampling, and reliance on binary comparisons, and asks whether these methods reflect real user expectations.

Method: The paper analyzes 1,500 open-ended responses from the PRISM dataset covering 75 countries, examining how people describe desired AI values, capabilities, and behavioral expectations beyond binary preference comparisons.

Result: The authors find strong heterogeneity in preferences: most values are requested by fewer than 25% of respondents, truthfulness is the only widely shared value at 49% yet is interpreted in incompatible ways, some traits like human-likeness and guardrails are explicitly contested, and users often distinguish between default behavior and requested behavior in ways binary comparisons cannot represent.

Conclusion: Current RLHF-based alignment flattens diverse, context-dependent, and sometimes incompatible human preferences into single reward models, which fails to capture what people actually want from AI systems.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are often fine-tuned through Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) to align with people's preferences and values. However, this method has known limitations: it aggregates conflicting preferences, often relies on unrepresentative samples, and uses only binary comparisons. Analysing 1,500 open-ended responses from the PRISM dataset across 75 countries, we examine what people actually want from AI systems and reveal concrete failures of current methods.
  We find that different people want different things: most values are requested by fewer than a quarter of respondents, with truthfulness the sole exception at 49%. Furthermore, the same words hide divergent meanings: when people describe what they mean by "truthfulness", they reveal distinct, potentially incompatible, epistemological bases, as some ask for sourced claims, some for expert opinions, and some even ask for unpopular views. Certain capabilities, namely how human-like a model behaves, and some features, like AI guardrails, are outright controversial, with some desiring them and others rejecting them. We additionally find that people often use contextual distinctions (what AI should do "by default" versus "if requested") that binary comparisons cannot capture.
  These findings expose fundamental problems in current alignment practices. When 49% request truthfulness but define it differently, this is unlikely to be captured by a single reward model. The persistence of high hallucination rates in well-funded models, despite users' clear demands for accuracy, suggests that current methods fail to identify actual preferences. This paper sheds light on the situated, contested, imperfect signals that are currently being flattened into universal preference models, a practice others have characterised as epistemic violence.

</details>


### [98] [HKJudge: A Legal Discourse-Annotated Corpus for Interpreting What Courts Find, How They Reason, and What They Rule](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06679)
*Xi Xuan,Wenxin Zhang,Yufei Zhou,King-kui Sin,Chunyu Kit*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper releases HKJudge, the first expert-annotated sentence-level discourse corpus for Hong Kong criminal judgments, defines two legal NLP tasks on it, and benchmarks BERT and LLM models to establish a foundation for future legal judgment modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior discourse analysis of Hong Kong court judgments has been limited because no expert-annotated corpus existed. The authors aim to fill this resource gap and support computational modeling of how courts state facts, reason, and rule.

Method: The paper constructs HKJudge by collecting criminal judgments from all five levels of Hong Kong courts and annotating them with a two-tier discourse schema. Ten legal linguistics experts annotate 290k sentences with 26 rhetorical roles at sentence level and three sentencing elements at span level, then benchmark BERT-based models, open-source LLMs, and commercial LLMs on rhetorical role classification and legal element extraction.

Result: HKJudge contains about 290k sentences and 6.5 million tokens with strong annotation quality (inter-annotator agreement kappa = 0.8). The paper also provides the first benchmark results across multiple model families for two legal NLP tasks on this dataset.

Conclusion: HKJudge is the first large-scale, sentence-level expert-annotated discourse dataset for Hong Kong criminal judgments, enabling robust study of judicial discourse structure and supporting benchmark tasks for legal NLP.

Abstract: Court judgments are central to legal practice and jurisprudence, yet discourse analysis of Hong Kong judgments has received limited attention, owing largely to the absence of expert-annotated corpora. We introduce the Hong Kong Judgment Discourse Dataset (HKJudge), the first sentence-level expert-annotated legal discourse corpus. HKJudge includes criminal judgments across all five levels of HK's court hierarchy, comprising $\sim$290k sentences and $\sim$6.5 million tokens, fully annotated by legal linguistics experts. We design a two-tier discourse schema that captures what facts a court finds, how it reasons, and what it rules. At the sentence level, each sentence is assigned one of 26 rhetorical roles. At the span level, sentences are further annotated with three sentencing elements (charge, imprisonment term, fine). Ten legal linguistics annotators produced the annotations with an inter-annotator agreement of $κ= 0.8$. We formulate two tasks on HKJudge, termed rhetorical role classification and legal element extraction, and provide the first benchmark evaluation of four BERT-based models, two open-source LLMs under zero-shot and fine-tuning settings, and four commercial LLMs on both tasks. Our work demonstrates the value of sentence-level discourse annotation for modeling the structure of HK judgments and provides a rich data foundation for future work on legal judgment prediction. The HKJudge dataset and code are available at https://github.com/xuanxixi/HKJudge.

</details>


### [99] [Signal-Driven Observation for Long-Horizon Web Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06708)
*Shubham Gaur,Ian Lane*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Instead of rereading the entire page at every action, web agents should use a signal-triggered module that compresses the page into only relevant elements, reducing context overload and improving long-horizon reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current web agents repeatedly ingest very large raw DOM and accessibility trees at each step, which wastes context budget and causes reasoning quality to degrade before long tasks are completed.

Method: It proposes Signal-Driven Observation (SDO), where a dedicated sub-call reads the full DOM/accessibility tree, extracts only task-relevant elements and selectors, and refreshes this compact observation only when specific signals occur, such as URL changes, new visible interactive elements, action failures, or browser events.

Result: As an abstract-level position/proposal paper, it does not report quantitative experimental results here; instead, it introduces the SDO design, frames the associated open research problems, and argues for a shift in web-agent architecture.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that tying observation updates to every action is a flawed design for long-horizon web agents, and that observation compression via selective, signal-triggered DOM reading should be treated as a first-class architectural decision.

Abstract: Web agents operating over long horizons ingest raw DOM and accessibility trees -- routinely tens of thousands of tokens -- at every action step, causing progressive context degradation that erodes reasoning well before tasks complete. We argue that this coupling of observation frequency to action frequency is an architectural mistake. Drawing on the insight from Recursive Language Models that querying a document outperforms reading it wholesale, we propose Signal-Driven Observation (SDO): a dedicated sub-call reads the full DOM but returns only task-relevant elements and their selectors, and is re-invoked only when a lightweight signal detector fires -- triggered by URL transitions, newly visible interactive elements, action failures, or exogenous browser events. We outline the open problems SDO introduces and call on the community to treat observation compression as a core architectural decision in web agent design.

</details>


### [100] [Data-Efficient Autoregressive-to-Diffusion Language Models via On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06712)
*Xingyu Su,Jacob Helwig,Shubham Parashar,Atharv Chagi,Lakshmi Jotsna,Degui Zhi,James Caverlee,Dileep Kalathil,Shuiwang Ji*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Transform an ARLM into a DLM with on-policy self-distillation so the new model trains on its own inference trajectories and inherits the frozen ARLM's knowledge, achieving strong results with far less training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior ARLM-to-DLM transformation methods suffer from two distribution shifts: loss of knowledge when switching from next-token prediction to a diffusion objective, and train-inference mismatch because DLMs are trained on random masks but decoded on confidence-based trajectories.

Method: The paper replaces causal attention with bidirectional attention in an autoregressive model and trains the transformed model using self on-policy distillation: the student model generates its own decoding trajectories, while the original frozen ARLM provides target logits on those trajectories.

Result: Empirical results show strong performance across many tasks while requiring 15x to 7,000x fewer training tokens than prior approaches, making DLM transformation practical as post-training rather than full pretraining.

Conclusion: OPDLM shows that autoregressive language models can be transformed into diffusion language models efficiently through on-policy distillation, avoiding expensive DLM pretraining while retaining much of the original ARLM knowledge.

Abstract: We study the transformation of autoregressive models (ARLMs) into diffusion language models (DLMs). Rather than pretraining from scratch, prior work replaces the causal attention in ARLMs with bidirectional attention and then trains the resulting model using a DLM objective. However, these approaches incur two distribution shifts. First, transitioning from a next-token prediction objective to a DLM objective can discard knowledge acquired by the ARLM during training. Second, standard DLMs suffer from a train-inference mismatch, as the training loss is defined on randomly masked sequences rather than the trajectories encountered at inference produced by confidence-based decoding. To address both challenges, we introduce an On-Policy Diffusion Language Model (OPDLM) in which On-Policy Distillation (OPD) is employed for ARLM-to-DLM transformation. Specifically, OPDLM is trained via self-OPD, where the student, an ARLM with bidirectional attention, generates its own trajectories, and the teacher, the original frozen ARLM, distills its knowledge by providing target logits on these trajectories. By training directly in an on-policy manner, OPDLM eliminates the train-inference mismatch in DLMs, while distillation from the original model enhances knowledge retention from the ARLM. Empirical results demonstrate that OPDLM requires 15x to 7,000x fewer training tokens with strong performance across a wide variety of tasks. OPDLM avoids the prohibitive cost of DLM pretraining and positions DLM transformation as a form of ARLM post-training.

</details>


### [101] [Does Topic Sentiment Cause Perceived Ideology? Comparing Human and LLM Annotations in Political News Articles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06715)
*Upasana Chatterjee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: High F1 does not guarantee human-like causal structure: a fine-tuned LLM can learn a false sentiment-ideology shortcut that looks valid in classification but distorts downstream causal analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper is motivated by the need to test whether topic sentiment causally affects perceived ideology and whether this relationship changes depending on whether ideology labels come from humans or LLMs used as annotation proxies.

Method: The study compares ideology labels from human experts, baseline and fine-tuned GPT-4o-mini, and Llama-3.3-70B on AllSides articles with shared sentiment annotations, then applies Double Machine Learning and community-level mediation analysis across annotator paradigms.

Result: Human annotations show no significant community-level causal effects, while fine-tuned GPT-4o-mini achieves the best F1 score (72.48) and uniquely produces significant community-level treatment effects and significant natural direct effects in mediation.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that fine-tuned GPT-4o-mini internalizes a spurious sentiment-ideology association that appears in causal and mediation analyses but not in human annotations, revealing a shortcut-learning failure mode that standard F1 evaluation does not detect.

Abstract: We ask whether topic sentiment has a causal effect on perceived political ideology, and whether the answer depends on who assigns the ideology label. Using articles from AllSides, paired with shared sentiment annotations from Llama-3.3-70b-versatile, we compare ideology labels from expert human annotators, GPT-4o-mini (baseline and finetuned), and Llama-3.3-70B. We apply Double Machine Learning (DML) and community-level mediation analysis across all four annotation paradigms. Human annotations yield no significant causal effects at the community level. Fine-tuned GPT-4o-mini achieves the highest classification accuracy (F1=72.48) and is the only annotator paradigm that produces significant community-level treatment effects and significant natural direct effects (NDEs) in mediation. We interpret this as evidence of shortcut learning: fine-tuning on ideology-labeled data causes the model to internalise a spurious sentiment--ideology coupling not operative in human judgment for this task. This coupling is structurally invisible to F1-based evaluation, with implications for the use of LLM annotations as silver labels and as proxies for human judgment in downstream causal analyses.

</details>


### [102] [Modular Monolingual Adaptation using Pretrained Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06738)
*Nalin Kumar,Ondřej Dušek*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Instead of finetuning an entire pretrained LM for a low-resource language, the paper swaps in target-language tokens, freezes their embeddings, and tunes the rest of the model. This simpler modular strategy yields better results on mask filling, NER, and POS for Scottish Gaelic, Irish, and Quechua.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-model finetuning is the standard way to adapt pretrained language models to low-resource languages, but it may be unnecessarily expensive or suboptimal. Since prior work suggests language-specific tokenization helps, the authors investigate whether a more modular, partial-tuning strategy can transfer knowledge more effectively.

Method: The paper adapts pretrained language models to low-resource languages by introducing a language-specific tokenizer/vocabulary, replacing token embeddings, freezing those corresponding embeddings, and finetuning the rest of the network. Experiments are conducted on Scottish Gaelic, Irish, and Quechua, and evaluated on mask filling, NER, and POS tasks.

Result: Across Scottish Gaelic, Irish, and especially very low-resource Quechua, the proposed method improves NLU performance over standard adaptation baselines. The study also analyzes how training strategy, pretrained embeddings, and model choice affect adaptation quality.

Conclusion: A modular adaptation strategy for low-resource monolingual language models, where target-language tokens are replaced, their embeddings are frozen, and the remaining model is tuned, can outperform full-model finetuning on several NLU tasks.

Abstract: Building monolingual language models (LMs) for low-resource languages typically relies on adapting pretrained language models (PLMs) by finetuning the whole model on the target language. This approach is widely favored over training from scratch, as it enables effective knowledge transfer. Additionally, prior work has shown that using a language-specific tokenizer can enhance the adaptability. In this work, we hypothesize that full model tuning is often unnecessary and propose a more modular approach. Specifically, we replace the tokens, freeze the corresponding embeddings, and tune the rest of the model. We use Scottish Gaelic, Irish, and Quechua for our experiments, with Quechua being a very low-resource language (8.5k training instances). Evaluation on natural language understanding (NLU) tasks -- mask filling, NER, and POS -- shows that our proposed approach improves performance when adapting models to low-resource languages. Additionally, we provide a comprehensive analysis of the effectiveness of training strategies, the choice of pretrained embeddings, and models.

</details>


### [103] [When to Think Deeply: Inhibitory Deliberation for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06745)
*Zhixuan He,Yue Feng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: IDPR lets an LLM answer fast by default, but blocks weak fast answers and sends only those cases to slow reasoning. This yields better accuracy with very limited extra computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Always using slow reasoning is expensive and unnecessary for many inputs. Existing input-only routing methods ignore useful evidence contained in the model's own fast response. The paper aims to reduce computational cost while preserving or improving accuracy by deciding based on the generated fast answer itself.

Method: The paper proposes IDPR, a response-conditioned inhibitory deliberation framework. It first generates a concise fast answer, then an inhibition controller decides whether to release that answer or suppress it and invoke slow reasoning. The controller uses features from the fast response and fast-side evidence, including confidence, logit margin, parseability, and generation cost. It is trained on paired fast-slow outcomes, and the inhibition threshold is chosen on a validation set under an accuracy-first budget.

Result: On a held-out 5,000-example mathematical reasoning test set, IDPR calls slow reasoning on only 8.20% of examples and increases accuracy from 47.90% to 48.92%. Under the same slow-call budget, random routing drops accuracy to 46.76%, and the best confidence-based baseline reaches 48.22%. IDPR also achieves the highest corrective precision.

Conclusion: IDPR improves mathematical reasoning accuracy by selectively triggering slow reasoning only when a fast response is likely unreliable, outperforming random and confidence-based routing under the same slow-call budget.

Abstract: Reasoning Large Language Models can improve problem-solving performance through deliberative inference, but invoking slow reasoning for every input is computationally expensive and often unnecessary. We propose IDPR, a framework for response-conditioned inhibitory deliberation. IDPR first generates a concise intuitive answer and then uses an inhibition controller to decide whether that specific response should be released or suppressed in favor of slow reasoning. Unlike input-only routers, the inhibition controller conditions on the fast answer and fast-side evidence, including confidence, logit margin, parseability, and generation cost. We train the controller from paired fast-slow outcomes and select the inhibition threshold on a held-out validation set under an accuracy-first slow-call budget. On a held-out 5,000-example mathematical reasoning test set, IDPR invokes slow reasoning on only 8.20% of examples and improves accuracy from 47.90% to 48.92%. Under the same slow-call budget, random routing decreases accuracy to 46.76%, while the strongest confidence-based baseline reaches 48.22%. IDPR also achieves the highest corrective precision, showing that response-conditioned inhibition better identifies fast answers that benefit from slow reasoning.

</details>


### [104] [Evidence Graph Consistency in Retrieval-Augmented Generation: A Model-Dependent Analysis of Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06748)
*Jianru Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) reduces but does not eliminate hallucination in large language models. Existing detection methods rely on flat similarity between generated answers and retrieved passages, ignoring structural relationships among evidence pieces and answer claims. We propose Evidence Graph Consistency (EGC), a framework that constructs a local evidence graph per response and computes five structural consistency measures as hallucination indicators. Evaluated on the full question answering split of RAGTruth across six LLMs (5,767 responses), EGC reveals a consistent model-family split: graph consistency features show the expected diagnostic direction for hallucinations in Llama-2 models but exhibit systematic reversal in GPT-4, GPT-3.5, and Mistral-7B. This reversal suggests qualitatively different hallucination patterns across model families and indicates that embedding-based graph consistency cannot serve as a model-independent hallucination detection signal.

</details>


### [105] [PromptPrint: Behavioral Biometrics Through Natural Language Prompting in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06755)
*Shaiv Patel,Kartik Narayan,Vishal Patel*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Short LLM prompts still reveal who wrote them. Surface word choice matters more than semantic intent, users are distinctive but context-variable, and paraphrasing is the most effective way to obscure identity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional authorship attribution mainly targets long-form expressive writing, while LLM interactions are usually short, task-oriented prompts. The paper asks whether such prompts still contain stable, identifiable, and distinctive personal signals that can be learned as a behavioral biometric.

Method: The paper introduces PromptPrint and studies authorship attribution on 20,680 real prompts from 1,034 users. It compares lexical representations with semantic encoders, analyzes stylometric distinctiveness versus cross-context consistency, and performs adversarial perturbation tests ranging from minor lexical edits to semantic paraphrasing.

Result: Lexical features significantly outperform semantic encoders, supporting a lexical stability hypothesis. Stylometric analysis shows a uniqueness-consistency paradox: users are distinctive overall but inconsistent across contexts. Adversarial tests find robustness to small lexical perturbations but substantial degradation under semantic paraphrasing, while overall identification performance remains strong at scale.

Conclusion: Prompt-based identity is a viable behavioral biometric: user prompts contain stable and distinctive author signals that enable strong identification at scale, but these signals are weakened by semantic paraphrasing, creating both utility and privacy risks.

Abstract: Authorship attribution research has traditionally focused on long-form, expressive texts; however, interactions with large language models (LLMs) are typically brief and task-driven prompts. This raises a fundamental question: do such prompts contain a stable, author-identifiable, and distinctive signal? We introduce PromptPrint, a systematic study of prompt-based identity, the hypothesis that a user's habitual vocabulary, syntax, and discourse patterns form a learnable behavioral biometric. Using 20,680 real prompts from 1,034 users, we establish three key findings. First, lexical representations significantly outperform semantic encoders, supporting the "lexical stability hypothesis": identity is primarily encoded in surface-level word choice rather than abstract intent. Second, stylometric features exhibit a "uniqueness-consistency paradox": users are highly distinctive across the population, yet behaviorally inconsistent across contexts. Third, adversarial analysis reveals a clear vulnerability spectrum: identity signals are robust to minor lexical perturbations but degrade substantially under semantic paraphrasing. Overall, our results demonstrate strong identification performance at scale, establishing prompt-based identity as a viable behavioral biometric. This work introduces a new perspective on user modeling in LLM interactions, with important implications for security and privacy. Data and code will be released upon the acceptance of our work.

</details>


### [106] [A Four-Condition Diagnostic Protocol for Evidence Utilization in Long-Context and Retrieval-Augmented Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06758)
*Haizhou Xia*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a diagnostic evaluation protocol, not a leaderboard metric, for testing whether LMs actually use given evidence. Using four matched evidence conditions and ONCU, it separates no-evidence answerability, oracle-evidence recoverability, full-context utilization, and retrieval-conditioned utilization, and shows that different tasks expose different bottlenecks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is that existing evaluation signals cannot distinguish whether a model answered from memory, ignored correct provided evidence, or cited evidence without actually using it to generate the requested answer. A more diagnostic framework is needed to isolate different failure modes in long-context and retrieval-augmented systems.

Method: The authors introduce a matched four-condition evidence-availability protocol with no evidence, full context, retrieved evidence, and oracle-evidence reference conditions, while holding prompts, examples, scoring fields, retrieval settings, and validity checks fixed. They use ONCU as a protocol-bound estimator of recovered oracle-reference evidence advantage on denominator-valid groups, and report denominator-free answer, evidence, retrieval, and failure-audit metrics separately.

Result: Across 18,000 ONCU-compatible predictions on Controlled-ONCU-safe16K, HotpotQA-ONCU, and 2WikiMultiHopQA-ONCU using five open-weight local models, the study finds a task-dependent bottleneck split. Controlled synthetic tasks mainly show failures in utilizing full context, whereas realistic multi-hop tasks mainly show failures in retrieval-chain coverage, and ONCU supports the same directional conclusion on oracle-improving groups.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that standard metrics like final-answer accuracy, retrieval recall, and citation overlap are insufficient to determine whether long-context or retrieval-augmented LMs truly used provided evidence. Its four-condition protocol and ONCU-based analysis show that evidence-utilization failures differ by task: synthetic settings mainly reveal full-context use failures, while realistic multi-hop QA mainly reveals retrieval-chain coverage failures.

Abstract: Final-answer accuracy, retrieval recall, and citation overlap do not by themselves identify whether a long-context or retrieval-augmented language model used the evidence it was given. A model can answer from parametric memory, fail despite receiving the right passages, or cite evidence without converting it into the requested answer. This paper proposes a matched four-condition evidence-availability protocol--no evidence, full context, retrieved evidence, and oracle-evidence reference--for diagnosing evidence utilization under fixed examples, prompts, score fields, retrieval settings, and validity checks. ONCU is used as a protocol-bound estimator of recovered oracle-reference evidence advantage and is computed only for denominator-valid groups; denominator-free answer, evidence, retrieval, and failure-audit metrics are reported separately. The empirical study evaluates five local open-weight models from the Qwen, Gemma, Llama, and Mistral families across Controlled-ONCU-safe16K, HotpotQA-ONCU, and 2WikiMultiHopQA-ONCU, with 18,000 ONCU-compatible predictions. The main finding is a task-dependent bottleneck split: controlled synthetic settings primarily expose full-context utilization failures, whereas the tested realistic multi-hop settings primarily expose retrieval-chain coverage failures in denominator-free answer and evidence metrics, with ONCU supporting the same direction on oracle-improving groups. The contribution is a diagnostic protocol for separating no-evidence answerability, oracle-evidence recoverability, full-context utilization, and retrieval-conditioned utilization, rather than a single-score leaderboard for long-context or retrieval-augmented systems.

</details>


### [107] [When Better Codebooks Are Not Enough: Predictive Performance and Behavioral Reliability in LLM Political Event Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06781)
*Zixian He,Bharath Raahul Murugesan,Patrick Brandt,Yibo Hu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LLMs get more accurate when codebooks are rewritten to be clearer, but they still may not follow the actual coding logic reliably. For codebook-based social-science tasks, evaluation should include behavioral reliability tests, not just accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many social-science workflows use expert codebooks to convert text into structured data, but high classification accuracy may not mean that an LLM is faithfully following the intended coding rules. The authors therefore examine whether LLMs truly internalize codebook logic.

Method: The paper studies political event coding as a source-target relation classification task, reformulates expert codebooks into more LLM-friendly versions with clearer definitions, examples, retrieved context, and edge-case rules, and then tests model behavior under controlled perturbations such as label renaming, codebook reordering, and remapping of label-definition pairs.

Result: Clearer, operationalized codebooks substantially improve classification accuracy, especially for fine-grained event labels. However, these gains do not consistently translate into behavioral reliability: models can still output plausible labels and even recover definitions while failing invariance-style tests under controlled codebook modifications.

Conclusion: Codebook-guided LLM systems should not be judged by accuracy alone; they must also preserve the underlying coding logic, because better-performing models can still be behaviorally unreliable under controlled codebook changes.

Abstract: High accuracy does not necessarily make an LLM a faithful coder. This issue matters because many social-science studies rely on expert-written codebooks to turn text into structured data. We study this problem in political event coding, a challenging source-target relation classification task beyond ordinary sentence-level classification, where models must determine what one actor did to another using detailed coding rules.
  We test whether expert codebooks become more effective when operationalized into LLM-friendly forms with clearer definitions, examples, retrieved context, and rules for difficult cases. We then evaluate behavioral reliability under controlled changes to label names, codebook order, and label-definition mappings. Clearer codebooks substantially improve classification performance, especially for fine-grained event classification. However, these predictive gains do not fully translate into behavioral reliability. Models may produce valid labels and recover definitions while still failing behavioral reliability tests under controlled codebook changes.
  These findings suggest that codebook-guided LLM systems should be evaluated not only by accuracy, but also by whether they preserve the coding logic that makes coded outputs meaningful for social-science research.

</details>


### [108] [Explain Like I'm 5 or Whatever I Choose: Evaluating the Interactive Potential of Language Model Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06788)
*Indu Panigrahi,Tal August*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces an interface-aware evaluation for scientific QA: can LLMs generate several answers to the same query at clearly different language-complexity levels? Across 98 queries, major models struggle to control complexity consistently, suggesting current LLMs are not yet reliable for direct-manipulation response interfaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM evaluations in scientific information seeking have become more use-centric, including live and multi-turn studies with real users, but they still largely assume a single static chat interface. As LLMs are embedded into new interfaces, evaluation must include criteria tied to those interfaces. The paper is motivated by the need to assess whether models can support direct-manipulation-style interfaces through controllable variation along interpretable response dimensions.

Method: The paper proposes an evaluation framework motivated by interface-specific assessment. Based on a formative study with 16 participants, it tests whether models can generate multiple responses to the same scientific query that differ along an interpretable linguistic axis: language complexity. The authors evaluate GPT-5.1, GPT-5 mini, Claude Sonnet 4.5 + Thinking, and DeepSeek-V3.1 by producing 5 responses at different complexity levels for 98 scientific queries, and they validate findings with larger samples and alternative complexity settings.

Result: All tested models can vary response complexity to some extent across multiple outputs, but these variations are mostly inconsistent. Claude Sonnet 4.5 performs best, yet it moves reliable complexity measures in the correct direction only 46% of the time. The main pattern remains stable under increased sample size and when using alternative complexity levels.

Conclusion: Current LLM evaluations for scientific information seeking need to account for interface-specific capabilities rather than assuming a single static chat interface. In a direct-manipulation-inspired setting where models generate multiple answers at graded language complexity levels, existing models show limited and inconsistent control; even the best model only reliably shifts complexity in the intended direction 46% of the time.

Abstract: Evaluations of large language models (LLMs) in scientific information seeking tasks have become increasingly use-centric, such as conducting live or multi-turn evaluations with real users. These evaluations still assume a single, static chat interface, but as models are integrated into new interfaces, evaluations must shift to incorporate interface-specific criteria. We propose a new evaluation framework based on a formative study with $16$ participants that tests models' ability to generate multiple responses to one query that differ along an interpretable axis of language (language complexity), inspired by direct manipulation interfaces from human-centered design literature. We evaluate GPT-5.1, GPT-5 mini, Claude Sonnet 4.5 + Thinking, and DeepSeek-V3.1 by generating 5 responses at different levels of language complexity for $98$ scientific queries. While models vary complexity across responses, most changes remain inconsistent, with the best performing model (Claude Sonnet 4.5) only shifting reliable complexity measures in the correct direction $46\%$ of the time. Our findings hold with increased sample size and alternative complexity levels.

</details>


### [109] [TA-RAG: Tone-Aware Retrieval-Augmented Generation for Peer-Support Health Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06794)
*Yong-Bin Kang,Anthony McCosker*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TA-RAG adds prompt-based tone control to RAG for sensitive HIV peer-support communication, improving empathy, readability, stigma-free language, and personalization without model fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RAG improves factual grounding, but in sensitive health peer-support settings such as HIV communication, factual correctness alone is not enough. Responses also need to be accessible, stigma-free, empathetic, and tailored to the recipient.

Method: The paper proposes TA-RAG, a lightweight prompt-based tone-aware RAG framework that adds explicit tone control to a standard RAG pipeline without fine-tuning. It operationalizes tone with four components: stigma-free rewriting, readability adjustment, recipient adaptation, and empathy rephrasing.

Result: Component-level evaluations based on HOLA-derived questions, UNAIDS terminology guidance, readability metrics, NAPWHA peer-support standards, and a public empathy dataset show that TA-RAG improves its targeted communication dimensions while preserving key content.

Conclusion: TA-RAG demonstrates that prompt-based tone control can make retrieval-augmented generation more suitable for sensitive peer-support health communication by improving communication quality while preserving factual content.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) successfully grounds large language model (LLM) outputs in trusted documents, but factual grounding alone is insufficient for sensitive peer-support health communication. In domains such as HIV peer support, responses must also be accessible, stigma-free, empathetic, and tailored to the recipient. This paper presents TA-RAG, a lightweight, prompt-based tone-aware RAG framework that embeds explicit tone control into a RAG pipeline without requiring model fine-tuning. We operationalise tone across four core components: stigma-free rewriting, readability adjustment, recipient adaptation, and empathy rephrasing. We evaluate TA-RAG through component-level tests using questions derived from HIV Online Learning Australia (HOLA), UNAIDS terminology guidance, readability metrics, peer-support standards from National Association of People with HIV Australia (NAPWHA), and a public empathy dataset. Results show that the TA-RAG's components improve their targeted communication quality while preserving key content. These findings emphasise that prompt-based tone control is a potential direction for making RAG outputs suitable for sensitive peer-support health communication.

</details>


### [110] [Korean Culture into LLM Alignment: Toward Cultural Coherence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06797)
*MinJae Jung,Minwoo Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Define Korean culturally coherent safety policies, generate preference data with frontier models, and DPO-tune open LLMs; this improves Korean cultural safety without significantly hurting general Korean performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior cultural alignment work for LLMs focuses mainly on suppressing harmful outputs; the paper argues that models also need a positive, working definition of what a culturally coherent response should look like in a specific cultural context such as Korean.

Method: The paper builds a Korean alignment-data pipeline using a prompt-based LLM seed generator to expand a Korean harm taxonomy, defines per-category Korean-culturally-adapted safe-response guidelines grounded in legal and social norms, collects candidate responses from three frontier models, and applies DPO fine-tuning on response triplets across six open-weight LLMs.

Result: DPO fine-tuning improves the Korean cultural safe rate across six open-weight LLMs, causes no large degradation on Korean general-capability benchmarks, and yields qualitatively better outputs that reference Korean statutes, institutional procedures, and constructive Korean-context information when appropriate.

Conclusion: A constructive definition of culturally coherent safe responses, instantiated for Korean, can be operationalized through an alignment-data pipeline and improves Korean cultural safety in open-weight LLMs without major loss in general capability.

Abstract: Cultural-aspect work on large language models is dominated by a negative target: which outputs to suppress. We argue that a constructive counterpart is also needed, a working definition of what a culturally coherent response is rather than only what it must avoid, and instantiate it for Korean. We design an alignment-data pipeline around a prompt-based LLM seed generator that expands a Korean harm taxonomy, with a Korean-culturally-adapted safe-response policy at its centre: a per-category guideline grounded in Korean legal frameworks, social norms, and interpretive conventions, against which three frontier models each produce a candidate response. DPO fine-tuning on the resulting triplets improves the Korean cultural safe rate across six open-weight LLMs while causing no large degradation on Korean general-capability benchmarks, and qualitative outputs show fine-tuned models naming Korean statutes and institutional procedures and, where appropriate, supplying constructive Korean-context information alongside refusal.

</details>


### [111] [Quantifying Media Representation Dynamics Across 25 Years of News Reporting on Policing-related Deaths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06812)
*Farhan Samir,Jappun Dhillon,Meghna Ravikumar,Syed Ishtiaque Ahmed,Vered Shwartz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Method: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Result: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Conclusion: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Abstract: We perform the largest known computational analysis of Canadian news narratives about police-involved deaths, spanning 4,000 articles from the last quarter-century. We develop a novel computational model, PerspectiveGap, grounded in prior sociological work on media representation of policing. We find that reporting on police-involved deaths on average features perspectives from state bureaucrats at a rate nearly three times as much as perspectives from other members of the public, including relatives, community members, eyewitnesses, lawyers representing the family, or civil liberties groups. A considerable fraction of articles contain no points of view from civilian actors, though civilian representation has increased in recent years. Qualitatively, we find that state bureaucrats' accounts of these deaths tend to be clinical and procedural, while civilian discourse carries considerably more emotional valence. The PerspectiveGap framework developed here can be contextualized to other jurisdictions, offering a scalable approach for analyzing how media systems construct narratives around policing and accountability.

</details>


### [112] [Progress-SQL: Improving Reinforcement Learning for Text-to-SQL via Progressive Rewards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06825)
*Shihao Zhang,Xiaoman Wang,Yuan Liu,Yunshi Lan,Weining Qian*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 论文针对Text-to-SQL中的多轮SQL修正问题，提出一种带渐进式奖励的强化学习方法，用结构诊断树和多种奖励信号更细粒度地评估“每一步是否更接近正确SQL”，最终在多个基准上取得稳定提升。 / The paper tackles multi-turn SQL refinement in Text-to-SQL with an RL framework that uses progressive, fine-grained rewards to judge whether each step gets closer to the correct SQL, yielding consistent improvements across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 现有Text-to-SQL强化学习通常只基于单个SQL状态的一次性奖励，无法为多轮SQL修正提供足够指导，也难以衡量逐步改进过程。 / Existing RL for Text-to-SQL usually relies on one-shot rewards over a single SQL state, which gives weak guidance for multi-turn correction and fails to capture stepwise improvement.

Method: 提出Progress-SQL框架：用Oracle-guided Diagnostic Tree将SQL抽象为子句级结构画像并生成诊断反馈；结合结构对齐与词汇对齐定义渐进式奖励；再加入progression latency reward和execution status reward，以鼓励更早达到正确SQL并从无效SQL中恢复。 / The paper proposes Progress-SQL: an Oracle-guided Diagnostic Tree abstracts SQL into clause-level structural profiles and provides diagnostic feedback; structural and lexical alignment are combined into a progressive reward; progression latency and execution status rewards are added to encourage earlier correctness and recovery from invalid SQL.

Result: 在BIRD、Spider及Spider鲁棒性变体上的实验表明，该方法在主评测和鲁棒性评测中都持续优于现有方法。 / Experiments on BIRD, Spider, and Spider robustness variants show consistent gains on both main and robustness evaluations.

Conclusion: Progress-SQL通过多轮强化学习和渐进式奖励，稳定提升了Text-to-SQL在标准与鲁棒性评测上的表现，说明针对SQL修正过程设计密集奖励是有效的。 / Progress-SQL improves Text-to-SQL consistently on standard and robustness benchmarks, showing that dense process-aware rewards are effective for iterative SQL correction.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning has recently shown promise in improving large language models for Text-to-SQL generation, yet existing methods typically optimize one-shot rewards defined over a single SQL state. Such rewards provide limited guidance for iterative SQL correction and are insufficient to capture the improvement of multi-turn SQL refinement. In this paper, we propose Progress-SQL, a multi-turn reinforcement learning framework with progressive rewards for Text-to-SQL. Our approach introduces an Oracle-guided Diagnostic Tree (ODT), which abstracts SQL queries into clause-level structural profiles and produces diagnostic feedback for next-turn refinement. To provide dense and robust reward signals, we combine ODT-based structural alignment with lexical alignment and define a progressive reward that measures the improvement from the initial SQL to the final SQL. We further incorporate a progression latency reward that favors earlier correctness and an execution status reward that encourages recovery from the invalid SQL. Experiments on BIRD, Spider, and Spider robustness variants demonstrate that our method consistently improves Text-to-SQL performance across both primary and robustness evaluations.

</details>


### [113] [The Dark Regulome: Disentangling Predictability from Regulation in Genomic Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06834)
*Chahat Baranwal,Aadtya Baranwal,Lakshya Nitin Tandon*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a diagnostic to tell apart sequence-predictability artifacts from real regulatory signal in foundation-model-based mutagenesis of glioma noncoding DNA. It finds a real ~10 kb local regulatory zone, shows that language-model rankings are heavily confounded by predictability, identifies Enformer as the only model retaining clear residual regulatory discrimination, and demonstrates that several striking biological patterns disappear under proper permutation controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-grade gliomas form functional synapses with neurons, implying that noncoding regulatory elements may shape synaptogenic gene expression in tumor cells. However, interpreting sequence foundation model scores in noncoding regions is difficult because likelihood-based scoring is confounded by sequence predictability. The work is motivated by the need to distinguish genuine regulatory biology in the dark regulome from artifacts of model-specific sequence modeling.

Method: The authors analyze 30,448 noncoding dark-genome elements across 92 glioma-relevant loci using in-silico mutagenesis with three distinct sequence foundation models: Caduceus-Ph, HyenaDNA, and Enformer. They introduce a residualization-and-permutation diagnostic to decompose RIS variance into predictability-driven versus regulation-driven components, compare model outputs across architectures, benchmark against a six-feature linear baseline, and validate surviving signals using conservation, brain cis-eQTL overlap, STRING-PPI, and empirical permutation tests.

Result: A sharp proximal-regulatory horizon of approximately 10 kb is consistently observed across all controls and models. However, the element-class ranking produced by language-model-derived scores is not robust, and a simple six-feature linear model predicts Caduceus top-decile membership with AUC = 0.985, indicating substantial non-biological structure. Cross-model analysis separates a predictability layer, shared by the two language models and enriched for long, well-predicted transposable elements, from a regulatory-output layer, where only Enformer retains residual cCRE-discriminative signal. Top-100 elements from each model show 3.3-fold enrichment for matching brain eQTLs per model with empirical p < 5×10^-3, while the putative transposable-element regulatory signal and NRXN1+NLGN1 protein-pair convergence fail corrected permutation testing.

Conclusion: The study concludes that a robust ~10 kb proximal regulatory horizon around glioma-relevant loci is consistently detectable across sequence foundation models, but much of the apparent signal from language-model-based in-silico mutagenesis is explained by local sequence predictability rather than true regulatory function. After residualization and permutation controls, Enformer retains unique cCRE-discriminative regulatory signal, while several initially appealing findings, including a transposable-element regulatory layer and NRXN1+NLGN1 convergence, do not survive proper permutation testing.

Abstract: High-grade gliomas integrate into neural circuits through functional synapses with neurons, raising the question of which noncoding elements shape synaptogenic gene expression in tumor cells. The regulatory program written across the dark genome, what we call the $\textit{dark regulome}$, is the natural substrate to probe, and sequence foundation models offer a zero-shot route through in-silico mutagenesis (ISM); yet likelihood-based scoring is tautologically coupled to local sequence predictability, leaving the regulatory interpretation underdetermined. Across three architecturally distinct foundation models (Caduceus-Ph, HyenaDNA, Enformer) and 30,448 dark genome elements at 92 glioma-relevant loci, we introduce a residualization-and-permutation diagnostic that separates predictability-driven from regulation-driven RIS variance. A sharp 10kb proximal-regulatory horizon survives every control we apply, but the LM-derived element-class hierarchy does not: a six-feature linear baseline matches Caduceus top-decile membership at AUC $= 0.985$. Cross-architecture decomposition cleanly separates a sequence-predictability layer (the two language models co-rank long well-predicted transposable elements) from a regulatory-output layer (Enformer alone retains residual cCRE-discriminative signal), with literally zero overlap between the two top-100 lists. Conservation, brain cis-eQTL, and STRING-PPI cross-checks then anchor what biology survives: top-100 elements across all three models are $3.3\times$ enriched per model for matching brain eQTLs ($p_\mathrm{emp} < 5\times 10^{-3}$), while a tempting transposable-element regulatory layer and a striking NRXN1+NLGN1 protein-pair convergence both fail proper permutation tests once those tests are constructed. We deliver the diagnostic as a general methodological tool for any ISM-based regulatory study.

</details>


### [114] [Translate-R1: Cost-Aware Translation Tool Use via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06835)
*Pratik Jayarao,Chaitanya Dwivedi,Himanshu Gupta,Neeraj Varshney,Adithya M Devraj,Meet Vadera,Priyanka Nigam,Bing Yin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Train a single reward-learned gate that translates only when the model is unsure. It boosts multilingual performance, cuts translation cost, and generalizes to unseen languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs perform unevenly across languages. Translating inputs into the model's dominant language can recover capability, but translating everything is wasteful, and letting the model decide naively fails because it is often overconfident and skips translation when needed. Existing solutions rely on manual rules or external routers.

Method: Continue reinforcement learning on post-trained Qwen3-4B using data from an answer-preserving translation pipeline across 22 languages, 3 resource tiers, and 5 domains; introduce confidence-gated GSPO for cost-sensitive tool use.

Result: The gated policy improves reward over the baseline by +4.6 on High-resource, +23.5 on Low-resource, and +17.5 on XLow-resource languages. It matches full reward at 63% of the cost versus an always-translate policy, is Pareto-optimal across 87% of the cost-sensitivity range, improves +18.7 on two synthetic unseen languages, and transfers zero-shot to 9 held-out languages.

Conclusion: A learned confidence-gated translation policy can decide when to translate based on the model's own comprehension, improving multilingual performance while reducing unnecessary tool use and transferring zero-shot to held-out languages.

Abstract: The performance gap across languages in LLMs is well documented, and closing it natively requires pretraining or fine-tuning on corpora that, for most languages, do not exist. Translation offers an alternative: converting an input into the model's dominant language unlocks its full capabilities at once. Applying translation to every input, however, is wasteful for languages the model already handles, while leaving the choice to the model fails in the opposite way, as LLMs are overconfident and skip the tool even when they cannot understand the input. Prior work resolves this with language-specific rules, domain heuristics, language identifiers, or external routers, each requiring manual engineering. We instead learn a single policy that decides when to translate from reward alone, developing language- and domain-adaptive introspection that assesses its own comprehension and invokes translation only when it cannot solve a task natively.
  Using data built by our answer-preserving translation pipeline, we continue RL on the post-trained Qwen3-4B across 22 languages in 3 resource tiers (High, Low, XLow) and 5 domains, and introduce confidence-gated GSPO for cost-sensitive tool use. The gated policy lifts reward over the baseline by +4.6 on High, +23.5 on Low, and +17.5 on XLow. Against an unconstrained policy that almost always translates, it preserves full reward at 63% of the cost and is Pareto-optimal across 87% of the cost-sensitivity range. Additionally, to simulate behavior on a completely unseen language, we create 2 synthetic languages, where our gated policy improves +18.7 over the overconfident baseline that underutilizes the tool even on these incomprehensible inputs. The policy transfers zero-shot to 9 held-out languages, and we analyze how tool use emerges over training, per language and per domain.

</details>


### [115] [Characterize Then Distill: Mechanistic Reasoning in Large Output Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06840)
*Debjyoti Saha Roy,Byron C. Wallace,Javed A. Aslam*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 论文认为大规模标签选择任务中的推理主要分两步：先粗筛候选，再精细判断；抓住这两个环节做蒸馏，比普通蒸馏更有效。 / The paper argues that reasoning in extreme-label selection works in two steps: first shortlist broadly, then reason carefully over that shortlist; distilling this structure works better than standard distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 尽管现代推理模型在需要从数十万到数百万标签中选择少量相关标签的零样本任务上表现出色，但其成功机制并不清楚。理解其内部工作过程不仅有助于解释性能来源，也可能指导更高效的蒸馏与模型设计。 / Although modern reasoning models perform surprisingly well on zero-shot tasks that require selecting a few relevant labels from hundreds of thousands to millions of candidates, the mechanism behind this success is unclear. Understanding that mechanism can explain where the performance comes from and guide better distillation and model design.

Method: 作者从机制层面研究现代推理模型在零样本极大标签空间多标签任务中的行为，将推理过程刻画为两阶段：先进行大范围候选筛选，再对筛选结果做细粒度推理；随后在多个数据集上分别验证这两个阶段可被隔离、且彼此互补，并基于该刻画提出机制蒸馏策略。 / The paper mechanistically studies how modern reasoning models solve zero-shot multi-label tasks with extremely large label sets. It models reasoning as a two-phase process: broad candidate shortlisting followed by fine-grained reasoning over the shortlist. The authors then validate across multiple datasets that these phases can be isolated and are complementary, and use this characterization to design a mechanistic distillation approach.

Result: 实验表明，推理模型的两阶段过程在不同数据集上都能被观察和分离，并且“短名单筛选”与“细粒度推理”是互补的；基于该机制理解提出的蒸馏方法稳定优于传统蒸馏方法。 / The experiments show that the two-stage reasoning process can be observed and separated across datasets, and that shortlisting and fine-grained reasoning are complementary. A distillation method built on this mechanistic view consistently outperforms conventional distillation.

Conclusion: 将推理模型在超大标签空间多标签任务中的能力分解为“候选短名单筛选 + 对短名单进行细粒度推理”两阶段后，可以据此设计出更有效的机制蒸馏方法，并且其效果稳定优于标准蒸馏。 / By decomposing reasoning in extreme-label multi-label tasks into two stages—candidate shortlisting and fine-grained reasoning over the shortlist—the authors derive a mechanistic distillation strategy that consistently outperforms standard distillation.

Abstract: Modern reasoning models offer surprisingly strong zero-shot performance on challenging multi-label tasks that require selecting a small set of relevant options from hundreds of thousands to millions of candidate labels. We investigate how they achieve this mechanistically. We characterize reasoning as a two-phase process: A broad "shortlisting" of candidates followed by fine-grained reasoning over the resulting set. We provide evidence across a range of datasets that these steps can be isolated and are complementary. Using this characterization, we develop a mechanistic distillation strategy that consistently outperforms standard distillation.

</details>


### [116] [CRAFT: A Unified Counterfactual Reasoning Framework for Tabular Question Answering and Fact Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06842)
*Chenshuo Pan,Yu Zhao,Jie Zhang,Changzai Pan,Zhenhe Wu,Jiayi Liang,Yujie Mao,Shuangyong Song,Yongxiang Li,Zhongjiang He*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CRAFT improves table QA and fact verification by making LLMs reason along both original and counterfactual paths, then combining the evidence, which yields better accuracy and robustness than standard one-way reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Table reasoning is difficult for LLMs because it often requires multi-step inference over long, structured tables. Existing methods mainly use single-direction reasoning, which restricts exploration of alternative hypotheses and weakens performance on complex tasks.

Method: The paper proposes CRAFT, a unified counterfactual reasoning framework that converts tabular question answering and fact verification into a bidirectional verification process. It constructs both original declarative statements and counterfactual variants, extracts evidence from both reasoning paths, and combines them through a weighted integration mechanism to produce the final answer.

Result: CRAFT consistently outperforms representative baselines on WikiTQ and TabFact, with especially strong gains on complex question answering. It also reduces performance disparities across different backbone LLMs.

Conclusion: CRAFT demonstrates that bidirectional counterfactual reasoning can overcome the limitations of single-direction inference in table reasoning, leading to more reliable and principled structured reasoning for LLMs.

Abstract: Table reasoning remains challenging for large language models (LLMs), particularly in tasks that require multi-step inference over long and structured tables. Existing approaches predominantly rely on single-direction reasoning, which limits their ability to explore alternative hypotheses across tasks. In this work, we propose CRAFT, a unified Counterfactual Reasoning Framework that reformulates Tabular question answering and fact verification into a general bidirectional verification process. Our method explicitly constructs both declarative statements and their counterfactual variants. Evidence is then extracted from reasoning along both the original and counterfactual paths, and integrated via a weighted mechanism to arrive at the final answer. Experimental results show that our approach consistently surpasses representative baselines on table reasoning datasets such as WikiTQ and TabFact, achieving especially large improvements on complex question answering. Our framework also significantly mitigates performance gaps between different backbone LLMs. This indicates that counterfactual reasoning effectively overcomes the limitations of single-direction inference, guiding LLMs toward more discerning reasoning and establishing a more principled paradigm for structured reasoning tasks. Our code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.

</details>


### [117] [Interpreting Brain Responses to Language with Sparse Features from Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06857)
*Michael A. Lepori,Kendrick Kay,Greta Tuckute*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Use sparse, interpretable LM features plus surprisal to predict fMRI responses in language cortex; this recovers known effects, discovers people-related tuning, and suggests brain-LM alignment reflects meaningful general representations rather than arbitrary hidden states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work linking human language cortex to artificial language models is often criticized as comparing one black box to another. The study aims to make encoding models more interpretable and to test whether brain-model alignment reflects meaningful structure in LM representations.

Method: The paper replaces dense language-model hidden states with hierarchically organized sparse autoencoder features and explicitly adds surprisal as a predictor in an encoding model. It evaluates this framework on 7T fMRI data from eight participants listening to 200 diverse sentences.

Result: The framework recovers known voxel selectivities for processing difficulty and abstractness, identifies a reliable voxel population tuned to people-related content, finds shared predictive features across the fronto-temporal language network, and shows frontal regions can be explained relatively well by surprisal alone. It also shows that the best brain predictions come from LM features encoding general information.

Conclusion: Augmented Sparse Encoding Models reveal interpretable language-cortex features, show that frontal-temporal language regions share core predictive features, and indicate that brain-language model alignment is driven by general, non-arbitrary LM features rather than any random hidden representation.
