Abstract: A central goal of cognitive neuroscience is to characterize the features that are represented by human language cortex. Artificial language models (LMs) have emerged as a powerful tool to address this challenge, but studies relating biological and artificial representations are often criticized as relating one black box to another. The present work introduces Augmented Sparse Encoding Models, an encoding framework that replaces dense LM hidden states with hierarchically-organized sparse autoencoder (SAE) features, while explicitly including surprisal as a predictor. Using this approach, we (i) produce interpretations of neural responses and (ii) test whether model-brain alignment reflects primary or idiosyncratic variation in LM representations. Using a high-field 7T fMRI dataset of eight participants listening to 200 linguistically diverse sentences, we first validate our modeling framework by recovering previous interpretations of voxel populations tuned to processing difficulty and meaning abstractness. We then interpret a previously-uncharacterized (but reliable) voxel population and find that it is tuned to people-related content. Next, we show that the fronto-temporal human language network is predicted by a common set of features across its constituent regions, but find that frontal regions are relatively well-explained by surprisal alone, even in the absence of LM-based features. Finally, we show that brain responses during language processing are not merely predictable from an arbitrary set of LM features. Rather, brain responses are best explained by the features that tend to capture the most general information encoded in LM representations, suggesting a nontrivial correspondence between brain and LM language representation.

</details>


### [118] [Are Large Language Models Suitable for Graph Computation? Progress and Prospects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06865)
*Yuting Zhang,Yi Han,Kai Wang,Wei Ni,Angela Bonifati,Wenjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A survey paper that classifies LLM-based graph computation into executor and planner roles, finding current LLMs useful for small/simple graph tasks but not yet dependable for large or exact graph computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior surveys mainly cover graph learning, text-attributed graphs, or graph-language modeling, leaving a gap in understanding when and how LLMs can be used for graph computation and integrated into graph-solving pipelines.

Method: The paper conducts a comprehensive survey of LLMs for graph computation and organizes existing methods using a role-based taxonomy with two paradigms: LLMs as executors and LLMs as planners.

Result: The review categorizes current approaches, analyzes their strengths and limitations, summarizes available datasets, and identifies four future research directions.

Conclusion: LLMs can assist graph computation effectively in limited settings, especially for simple and small-scale tasks, but they are still unreliable for large-scale problems and tasks requiring exact correctness.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have been increasingly explored for graph computation, where tasks require reasoning over structured relationships and algorithmic operations. Yet, it remains unclear when LLMs can reliably support such computation and how they should be incorporated into graph-solving pipelines. Existing surveys at the intersection of LLMs and graphs primarily focus on graph learning, text-attributed graphs, or graph-language modeling. To bridge this gap, we provide a comprehensive review of LLMs for graph computation through a role-based taxonomy. Specifically, we identify two major paradigms: i) LLMs as executors, where models directly solve graph tasks from graph descriptions and instructions; and ii) LLMs as planners, where models formulate problems, decompose reasoning steps, and invoke external tools or agents for execution. Based on this taxonomy, we analyze the strengths and limitations of current methods. Our review indicates that LLMs are promising for simple, small-scale tasks, but remain unreliable for large-scale and exactness-demanding tasks. Finally, we summarize available datasets and suggest four future directions.

</details>


### [119] [An Expanded Synthetic Conversation Dataset for Multi-Turn Smishing Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06879)
*Carl Lochstampfor,Ayan Roy*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: By scaling and cleaning a synthetic multi-turn smishing dataset, the authors show that Longformer finally beats XGBoost, confirming that transformers need larger, higher-quality conversational corpora to realize their advantages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior results showed XGBoost outperforming transformer models on the smaller COVA dataset, likely due to input truncation and limited training data. The work is motivated by the need to test whether larger, cleaner conversational datasets can unlock transformers' contextual advantages for smishing detection.

Method: The paper expands the original COVA dataset to COVA-X with 10,985 synthetically generated conversations across eight elder-targeted scam categories using an improved generation pipeline. It retrains multiple classifiers, including XGBoost and transformer models, and performs quality-control, sensitivity, and scam-type-specific analyses to evaluate the impact of dataset expansion and refinement.

Result: On COVA-X, Longformer achieves 79.71% accuracy and 0.7786 macro F1, surpassing XGBoost's 78.43% accuracy and 0.7563 macro F1. The new pipeline also improves label correction rates from 49.8% to 3.9% and reduces virtual-kidnapping artifacts from 67.1% to 46.5%, with analyses showing scam-category-dependent performance patterns and improved label-relevant signal after cleanup.

Conclusion: COVA-X demonstrates that when trained on a substantially larger and cleaner multi-turn smishing corpus, transformer models, specifically Longformer, can outperform traditional TF-IDF + XGBoost baselines, validating the importance of dataset scale and quality for conversational scam detection.

Abstract: Our prior work introduced COVA, a synthetically generated multi-turn conversational smishing dataset of 3,201 labeled conversations, establishing baseline detection benchmarks across eight models. While XGBoost with TF-IDF features achieved the best performance, with 72.5\% accuracy and 0.691 macro F1, transformer models underperformed, which was attributed to input truncation and insufficient training data. We present COVA-X, an expanded dataset of 10,985 conversations spanning eight elder-targeted scam categories, produced by an improved generation pipeline addressing contamination, label mismatch, stage-direction bleed, and prompt-design failures from the first iteration. Retraining all classifiers on the expanded dataset yields the central finding of this work: Longformer now surpasses XGBoost on all evaluation metrics, achieving 79.71\% accuracy and 0.7786 macro F1 compared with 78.43\% and 0.7563 for XGBoost. This directly confirms that transformer models require larger conversational corpora to realize their contextual advantages. We additionally document a quality life-cycle including a 12.7$\times$ improvement in label correction rate, from 49.8\% to 3.9\%, an architectural intervention reducing virtual-kidnapping artifact rates from 67.1\% to 46.5\%, and a per-scam-type outcome analysis showing that scam categories modulate results in mechanism-consistent ways. A pre/post-cleanup sensitivity analysis confirms that dataset refinement recovers genuine label-relevant signal across all three classifier architectures.

</details>


### [120] [EASE-TTT: Evidence-Aligned Selective Test-Time Training for Long-Context Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06906)
*Xiaopeng Yuan,Zebin Wang,Suwen Wang,Zongxin Yang,Haohan Wang,Yushun Dong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: EASE-TTT retrieves relevant evidence, turns it into soft attention targets, and uses that signal to adapt query-side attention at test time, yielding the best long-context QA performance for small models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smaller language models struggle with long-context QA even when relevant evidence is already present. Prior within-context retrieval methods expose evidence but do not adapt attention allocation, while qTTT adapts the model but lacks explicit evidence localization. The paper aims to combine both strengths.

Method: The paper proposes EASE-TTT, a retrieval-augmented test-time training framework. It first retrieves evidence chunks within the full context, converts them into soft attention supervision over token positions, and then adapts query-side attention parameters while still generating the final answer from the original full context.

Result: Across six LongBench QA tasks and three small decoder-only language models, EASE-TTT achieves the best macro-average performance among compared methods, including full-context inference, retrieval-only approaches, and qTTT.

Conclusion: EASE-TTT demonstrates that evidence-aligned, query-side test-time adaptation improves long-context QA for small decoder-only language models, outperforming full-context inference, retrieval-only baselines, and qTTT on LongBench QA tasks.

Abstract: Long-context question answering (QA) remains challenging for smaller language models even when answer-bearing evidence is already present in the input. Existing within-context retrieval methods localize and expose candidate evidence chunks for the question, but they stop at input-level evidence exposure rather than adapting the query-side attention parameters that control how the model allocates attention over full-context positions. In contrast, lightweight test-time adaptation methods, such as query-only test-time training (qTTT), leave evidence localization unresolved because their generic span-level self-supervised objectives do not identify which context positions support the current answer. In this paper, we propose Evidence-Aligned SElective Test-Time Training (EASE-TTT), a within-context retrieval-augmented test-time training framework that converts selected evidence chunks into a soft attention supervision target over their token positions. Instead of replacing the full context with retrieved chunks, EASE-TTT uses the resulting attention target to guide query-side adaptation, with the adapted model generating the final answer from the original full context. Experiments on six LongBench QA tasks and three small decoder-only language models show that EASE-TTT achieves the strongest macro-average performance among full-context inference, retrieval-only baselines, and qTTT, supporting evidence-aligned test-time adaptation in long-context QA.

</details>


### [121] [ThinkBooster: A Unified Framework for Seamless Test-Time Scaling of LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06915)
*Vladislav Smirnov,Chieu Nguyen,Sergey Senichev,Minh Ngoc Ta,Ekaterina Fadeeva,Artem Vazhentsev,Daria Galimzianova,Nikolai Rozanov,Viktor Mazanov,Jingwei Ni,Tianyi Wu,Igor Kiselev,Mrinmaya Sachan,Iryna Gurevych,Preslav Nakov,Timothy Baldwin,Artem Shelmanov*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: ThinkBooster unifies test-time compute scaling for LLM reasoning with tools for implementation, benchmarking, deployment, and debugging, and shows that adaptive inference can improve reasoning performance with analyzable compute trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test-time compute scaling methods for LLM reasoning are fragmented, compared under inconsistent evaluation protocols, and lack systematic analysis of performance versus compute cost, making real-world adoption and fair comparison difficult.

Method: The paper introduces ThinkBooster, which includes a modular Python library for TTC scaling strategies and reasoning scorers, a benchmark evaluating both accuracy and computational efficiency, an OpenAI-compatible proxy service for deployment, and a visual debugger for reasoning trajectory inspection.

Result: Experiments on mathematical and coding tasks show clear performance-compute trade-offs across TTC strategies and scoring methods, and demonstrate that ThinkBooster can deliver practical gains in realistic applications.

Conclusion: ThinkBooster provides a unified and practical framework for test-time compute scaling in LLM reasoning, making it easier to compare, deploy, and inspect TTC strategies while exposing their quality-cost trade-offs.

Abstract: Test-time compute (TTC) scaling has emerged as a powerful paradigm for improving large language model (LLM) reasoning by allocating additional compute during inference, e.g., via multi-sample generation and verifier-based reranking. Existing TTC scaling strategies and reasoning scorers remain fragmented, evaluated under inconsistent protocols, and are rarely analyzed through the lens of quality-cost trade-offs. We introduce ThinkBooster, a unified framework for seamless test-time compute scaling of LLM reasoning, which consists of (i) a modular Python library implementing state-of-the-art TTC scaling strategy and scorer families, (ii) a benchmark that jointly evaluates performance and computational efficiency, and (iii) a deployable OpenAI-compatible proxy service that enables drop-in integration of adaptive reasoning into real-world applications. We further provide a demo visual debugger for inspecting the reasoning trajectories, intermediate selection decisions, and alternative reasoning paths. Empirical results on mathematical and coding tasks reveal the performance-compute trade-offs of TTC scaling strategies and scoring methods and demonstrate that ThinkBooster provides practical gains in real-world tasks. The code is available online under an MIT license.

</details>


### [122] [Didact: A Cross-Domain Capability Discovery System for Defence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06942)
*Aarya Bodhankar,Aditya Joshi,Bao Gia Doan,Thomas Marchant,Oscar Leslie,Flora Salim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Method: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Result: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Conclusion: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Abstract: Policymakers in defence and defence-aligned sectors must monitor rapidly evolving research alongside sector priorities relevant to operational and strategic needs. In practice, these sources are fragmented across heterogeneous formats, disjoint repositories, and siloed update streams, making capability discovery slow and difficult to audit. We present Didact, a prototype that integrates publicly available defence reports and policy documents from Australia with a purpose-built knowledge graph derived from Australian research publications. Didact provides natural language conversations for policy-oriented workflows, and leverages a composite retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline. A key feature of Didact is an interactive Evidence Rail that visualises retrieved evidence and source relationships. Our evaluation of the output quality and runtime of Didact highlights its utility. While Didact has been co-developed as an academia-industry project for the Australian context, it is adaptable to other domains where knowledge is similarly fragmented. A demonstration video is available here:

</details>


### [123] [Auditing Training Data in Domain-adapted LLMs: LoRA-MINT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06946)
*Gonzalo Mancera,Daniel DeAlcala,Aythami Morales,Julian Fierrez,Ruben Tolosana,Francisco Jurado*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LoRA-MINT uses perplexity-based membership inference to audit whether data were used to fine-tune LoRA-based LLMs, reaching 0.77-0.92 precision and beating prior methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by the need to audit fine-tuned LLMs for intellectual property protection, sensitive data management, transparency, and responsible AI deployment by checking whether particular data samples were used during training.

Method: The paper proposes LoRA-MINT, a membership inference testing framework for LoRA-adapted LLMs. It analyzes the relationship between model perplexity and membership status to estimate whether a sample was part of the model's fine-tuning dataset.

Result: Experiments on four models and three benchmark datasets achieved precision between 0.77 and 0.92 for identifying training-set membership, outperforming state-of-the-art baselines and showing robustness and generality.

Conclusion: LoRA-MINT is an effective and scalable auditing framework for determining whether specific samples were included in the training data of LoRA-fine-tuned LLMs, and its methodology can likely generalize to other adaptation methods and domain-adapted AI models.

Abstract: We present LoRA-MINT, a new methodology for Membership Inference Test (MINT) applied to recent Large Language Models (LLMs) fine-tuned for specific Natural Language Processing (NLP) tasks through Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). The primary goal is to assess whether individual samples were part of the training data of these adapted models, providing a useful auditing tool for the management of intellectual property and sensitive data. Our analysis explores the relationship between model perplexity and membership status, providing a systematic framework for estimating data exposure in fine-tuned LLMs. We conducted experiments on four models and three benchmark datasets, obtaining precision values in determining if given data were used for training ranging from 0.77 to 0.92, which outperform state-of-the-art baselines and demonstrate the robustness and generality of the proposed method. In general, our findings underscore the potential of LoRA-MINT as an effective and scalable framework for auditing LLMs, improving transparency, and fostering the ethical and responsible deployment of AI and NLP technologies. For the sake of concreteness and current relevance, our discussion and experiments are centered on LoRAadjusted LLMs, but note that most of the presented methodology is easily applicable for auditing training data given any other technique for adapting LLMs or, more generally, any other domain-adapted AI models.

</details>


### [124] [OpenHalDet: A Unified Benchmark for Hallucination Detection across Diverse Generation Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06959)
*Xinyi Li,Zhen Fang,Yongxin Deng,Jinyuan Luo,Hongnan Ma,Changdae Oh,Zijing Shi,Shanshan Ye,Hanchen Wang,Shu-Lin Chen,Yadan Luo,Mengyue Yang,Sean Du,Sharon Li,Ling Chen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: OpenHalDet is a unified benchmark for evaluating LLM hallucination detectors under consistent settings across many tasks and access levels, making results more comparable, reproducible, and extensible.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing hallucination detection evaluations suffer from inconsistent inference/evaluation settings and narrow downstream coverage, making reported results hard to compare, reproduce, and generalize.

Method: The paper introduces OpenHalDet, a standardized evaluation pipeline covering prompt construction, response generation, truthfulness annotation, detector scoring, and metric computation. It supports black-box, gray-box, and white-box hallucination detectors within a shared framework across diverse generation scenarios.

Result: The main outcome is the release of an open benchmark and codebase that integrates diverse tasks, models, and detector families, allowing controlled comparison and systematic analysis of hallucination detection paradigms in LLM applications.

Conclusion: OpenHalDet provides a unified, open, and extensible benchmark that standardizes hallucination detection evaluation across models, tasks, and detector access settings, enabling more reproducible and comparable assessment of detector behavior.

Abstract: Hallucination detection is essential for the reliable deployment of large language models (LLMs). However, existing evaluations face two core challenges: inconsistent inference configuration and evaluation, and limited coverage of downstream domains and tasks. Consequently, reported detector performance is often difficult to compare, reproduce, and generalize beyond specific experimental settings. We introduce OpenHalDet, a unified benchmark for hallucination detection across diverse generation scenarios. OpenHalDet standardizes the evaluation pipeline, from prompt construction and response generation to truthfulness annotation, detector scoring, and metric computation. It supports heterogeneous detector families under different access settings, including black-box methods that use only generated outputs, gray-box methods that rely on probability-based signals, and white-box methods that exploit internal model signals. By bringing diverse tasks, models, and detectors into a shared framework, OpenHalDet enables controlled comparison and provides a systematic view of how different detection paradigms behave in LLM applications. We release OpenHalDet as an open and extensible codebase to facilitate reproducible evaluation and future development of hallucination detection methods. The code and datasets are available at https://github.com/Nellie179/Hallucination-Detection.

</details>


### [125] [Tree-of-Experience: A Structured Experience-Management Solution for Self-Evolving Agents under Low-Repetition and Implicit-Reward Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06960)
*Zihao Deng,Yining Zhu,Leiming Wang,Jingfei Lu,Junbo Wang,Chuncheng Ran,Yu Yang,Dixuan Yang,Jikun Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper builds a harder benchmark for agent self-evolution under implicit rewards and shows that a structured experience tree (ToE) works better than generic memory approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-evolution benchmarks for LLM agents usually assume explicit goals, repetitive task structures, and clear feedback, which do not reflect harder real-world settings where tasks repeat less, rewards are implicit, and feedback is delayed, noisy, and only available at the outcome level.

Method: The paper introduces FinEvolveBench, a temporally controlled benchmark for financial sentiment prediction that maps daily news-based predictions to future excess returns, and proposes Tree-of-Experience (ToE), a framework that structures agent experience through organization, retrieval, validation, and updating.

Result: Experiments show that general-purpose experience methods fail to consistently beat no-experience baselines, whereas ToE achieves better overall performance on the benchmark.

Conclusion: Structured experience management is essential for self-evolving LLM agents in implicit-reward, low-repetition environments; the proposed ToE method delivers stronger and more consistent performance than general-purpose experience mechanisms or no-experience baselines.

Abstract: Experience-based self-evolution is crucial for LLM agents, but existing benchmarks often assume explicit goals, stable task patterns, and clear feedback. We study a more challenging setting: low-repetition tasks with implicit rewards, where past experience is difficult to reuse and feedback is delayed, noisy, and outcome-level. We introduce \textsc{FinEvolveBench}, a temporally controlled benchmark for financial sentiment prediction that links daily news-driven predictions to future excess returns. We further propose Tree-of-Experience (ToE), a structured experience-management method that organizes, retrieves, validates, and updates agent experience. Experiments show that general-purpose experience mechanisms do not consistently outperform no-experience baselines, while ToE achieves stronger overall performance. These results highlight the importance of structured experience management for self-evolving agents in implicit-reward environments.

</details>


### [126] [Human Adults and LLMs as Scientists: Who Benefits from Active Exploration?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06464)
*Mandana Samiei,Eunice Yiu,Anthony GX-Chen,Dongyan Lin,Jocelyn Shen,Blake A. Richards,Alison Gopnik,Doina Precup*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Allowing adults to actively test hypotheses improves learning of conjunctive causes, weakening the classic conjunctive handicap, but conjunctive rules remain harder than disjunctive ones. Large language models can match human accuracy more closely than human exploration efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work shows a robust conjunctive handicap in causal learning: adults find conjunctive rules harder than disjunctive ones. However, most evidence comes from passive observation paradigms with limited data, so it remains unclear whether the bias reflects a deep inferential limitation or simply poor evidence conditions. The paper tests whether giving learners agency through active exploration changes this pattern.

Method: The study used a modified blicket detector task in which adult participants actively generated interventions to discover which objects were causal under either conjunctive or disjunctive rule structures. Performance was compared across rule types by measuring inference accuracy and the number or efficiency of tests used. The same task framework was also given to multiple large language models to compare their hypothesis inference and exploration behavior with humans.

Result: Adults performed substantially better on conjunctive causal reasoning when allowed to actively explore than has been reported in passive settings. Even so, conjunctive rules still required more interventions to identify than disjunctive rules. Among large language models, some strong models achieved near-human inference accuracy, but their exploration strategies were typically less efficient, and they also retained a conjunctive-disjunctive performance gap.

Conclusion: Active exploration reduces but does not eliminate the classic conjunctive handicap in adult causal learning. Adults can infer conjunctive causal rules more accurately when they can intervene freely, but they still require more evidence than in disjunctive settings. Large language models can approach human-level inference accuracy, yet they generally explore less efficiently and show similar conjunctive-versus-disjunctive asymmetries.

Abstract: A long-standing finding in the causal learning literature is that adults struggle to identify conjunctive causal rules, where an effect requires the simultaneous presence of multiple causes, while performing better in disjunctive settings. However, most demonstrations of this ``conjunctive handicap'' rely on passive observation paradigms with limited evidence, where learners have no control over evidence generation. This paper asks whether this bias persists when adults are granted agency through active exploration. Using a modified ``blicket detector'' task, adult participants freely intervened to identify causal objects under conjunctive or disjunctive rule structures. We show that active exploration substantially improves adults' conjunctive causal reasoning, although conjunctive rules still require more tests to infer than disjunctive rules. We further compare human performance to a range of large language models in the same setting. While some state-of-the-art models approach human-level performance on hypothesis inference accuracy, they often exhibit less efficient exploration strategies and similar conjunctive-disjunctive performance gaps.

</details>


### [127] [Contrastive Training with LLM-generated Near-Misses for Robust Code-Switching Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06985)
*Tung X. Nguyen,Hieu Minh Truong,Giang-Son Nguyen,Nhu Vo,Wray Buntine,Dung D. Le*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Detect code-switching hotspots, create hard near-miss negatives around them, and train Whisper with POI-weighted CE + contrastive loss; this improves mixed-language ASR by over 2% on two datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard ASR struggles with code-switching because errors concentrate around language-switch boundaries and difficult mixed-language regions; the work aims to train the model to better distinguish plausible but incorrect alternatives specifically at these critical spans.

Method: The paper detects code-switching point-of-interest spans, generates acoustically plausible hard negative hypotheses by perturbing POIs in ASR N-best outputs and expanding them with a large language model, filters negatives using acoustic/phonemic/textual constraints, and fine-tunes Whisper-small with LoRA using POI-weighted cross-entropy plus multi-negative contrastive ranking loss.

Result: On CS-FLEURS (cmn-eng) and ViMedCSS (vie-eng), the approach reduces both overall and code-switching-aware error rates by more than 2% compared with standard LoRA fine-tuning.

Conclusion: POI-aware contrastive training effectively improves code-switching ASR by focusing learning on CS-critical regions, yielding consistent gains over standard LoRA fine-tuning on two bilingual benchmarks.

Abstract: Code-switching (CS), the alternation between multiple languages within a single utterance, remains challenging for Automatic Speech Recognition (ASR). To address this issue, we propose a Point-of-Interest (POI)-aware contrastive training framework that improves recognition at CS-critical regions. We first identify CS spans by adopting POI detection method from literature, then construct acoustically plausible near-miss hypotheses by perturbing POIs in ASR N-best outputs and expanding candidates with a large language model. Hard but plausible negatives are retained through filtering with acoustic, phonemic, and textual constraints. Finally, we fine-tune Whisper-small with LoRA using a POI-weighted cross-entropy anchor objective together with a multi-negative contrastive ranking loss. Experiments on CS-FLEURS (cmn-eng) and ViMedCSS (vie-eng) show consistent reductions of over 2% in both general and CS-aware error rates compared to standard LoRA fine-tuning.

</details>


### [128] [Principles of Concept Representation in Sentence Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06994)
*Isabelle Mohr,John Dujany,Jonathan Souquet,Andre Freitas*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Ablation study shows sentence encoders represent concepts well only when latent geometry matches the concept’s compositional semantics; current fine-tuning mostly reshapes existing space, final-layer features are sufficient, hard negatives help calibration not ranking, and extensional supervision fails on relational/intensional concepts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to explain what makes a sentence encoder produce good concept representations, beyond aggregate benchmark performance. The paper studies this through representational compositionality to understand when encoder geometry matches the semantic structure required by different concept families.

Method: The authors run controlled ablations on sentence encoders trained with 3.3 million synonym and definition pairs from WordNet and Wiktionary. They evaluate on three decontaminated splits plus a modifier-labeled noun-phrase benchmark, and also introduce two new datasets for semantic-gap and NP paraphrase evaluation.

Result: The main results are four principles: fine-tuning mainly recalibrates latent geometry instead of expanding it; semantic signal is concentrated in the final transformer layer before concept-specific training, making cross-layer pooling unnecessary; hard negatives improve discrimination and robustness but not retrieval ranking; and extensional supervision helps intersective/subsective concepts while hurting relational/intensional ones.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that sentence encoder quality for concept representation is governed by representational compositionality: success depends on whether latent space can realize the relevant semantic operator with low distortion. It identifies four principles showing that current training paradigms are composition-type dependent and structurally limited for some concept families.

Abstract: What makes a sentence encoder produce good concept representations? We approach this through the lens of representational compositionality: an encoder supports a concept family only when its latent space admits a low-distortion realization of the corresponding semantic operator. This framing predicts both where current encoders succeed and where they are structurally mismatched to their supervision. Through a controlled ablation over encoder conditions trained on 3.3 million synonym and definition pairs from WordNet and Wiktionary, evaluated on three decontaminated splits and a modifier-labeled noun-phrase benchmark, we identify four principles. Fine-tuning recalibrates the latent geometry rather than expanding it (P1). Semantic signal concentrates in the final transformer layer before concept-specific training begins, making cross-layer pooling redundant (P2). Hard negatives improve discrimination and stress-test robustness without improving retrieval ranking, showing that calibration and ranking are independently addressable (P3). Finally, the effectiveness of supervision depends on the composition type of the target concept. Extensional training helps intersective and subsective families while degrading relational and intensional ones, exposing a structural limitation of current training paradigms (P4). We release two new evaluation datasets: a DBpedia semantic-gap benchmark and a modifier-labeled NP paraphrase suite.

</details>


### [129] [MADE: Beyond Scoring via a Multilingual Agentic Diagnosing Engine for Fine-Grained Evaluation Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07020)
*Yilun Liu,Miao Zhang,Shimin Tao,Minggui He,Chunguang Zhao,Chenxin Liu,Li Zhang,Chen Liu,Cheng Qian,Liqun Deng,Xiaojun Meng,Daimeng Wei*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MADE is an agentic framework for diagnosing multilingual benchmark results. By structuring analysis into staged reasoning and pairing it with an expert-designed multilingual diagnostic set, it produces much better diagnosis reports than prior baselines and turns benchmark scores into practical decisions and remediation insights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multilingual and multicultural benchmarks produce rich score tables across many languages and model families, but these outputs are hard to interpret deeply. Single LLMs or open-ended agents struggle with long, noisy diagnostic inputs, and there is no reusable taxonomy for fine-grained multilingual post-evaluation diagnosis.

Method: The paper proposes MADE, a Multilingual Agentic Diagnosing Engine that decomposes diagnosis into five stages: planning, aggregate analysis, instance-level case inspection, multilingual and cultural reflection, and grounded report synthesis. It is paired with an expert-designed diagnostic set of 54 queries across 15 languages and evaluated on a large multilingual evaluation substrate spanning many models, benchmarks, languages, cultures, and records.

Result: On a substrate with 33 model families, 11 benchmarks, 26 languages, 34 cultures, and 8.66M evaluation records, MADE outperforms the strongest shared baseline by 47% in diagnosis report quality and is preferred by human multilingual experts in 87.9% of pairwise comparisons. It also helps uncover four actionable findings related to deployment, iteration, and cross-cultural pitfalls.

Conclusion: MADE significantly improves multilingual post-evaluation diagnosis quality over strong baselines, is strongly preferred by human multilingual experts, and can convert large multilingual benchmark results into actionable guidance for model deployment, iteration, and cross-cultural risk mitigation.

Abstract: Multilingual and multicultural benchmarks now cover dozens of languages and model families, but the resulting score landscapes remain metric-rich and insight-poor, necessitating fine-grained multilingual post-evaluation diagnosis. However, single LLMs and open-ended agents are easily swamped by the long, noisy diagnostic input, and no reusable taxonomy exists for it. To address this, we propose MADE, a Multilingual Agentic Diagnosing Engine that decomposes post-evaluation analysis into planning, aggregate analysis, instance-level case inspection, multilingual and cultural reflection, and grounded report synthesis. MADE is paired with an expert-led 54-query and 15-language diagnostic set, evaluated on top of a large-scale multilingual evaluation substrate (33 model families, 11 benchmarks, 26 languages, 34 cultures, 8.66M evaluation records). Experiments show that MADE outperforms the strongest shared baseline by 47% in diagnosis report quality and is preferred by human multilingual experts in 87.9% of pairwise comparisons. Applied with multilingual experts, MADE further surfaces four actionable findings on deployment, iteration, and cross-cultural pitfalls, turning benchmark score tables into model-selection and remediation guidance.

</details>


### [130] [Beyond Rubrics: Exploration-Guided Evaluation Skills for Reward Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07040)
*Xing Yue,Linjuan Wu,Daoxin Zhang,Yongliang Shen,Weiming Lu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Eval-Skill precomputes compact, reusable evaluation skills for each domain and inserts them into the judge’s context, avoiding per-query rubric generation while significantly improving reward-modeling performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-ended reward modeling lacks verifiable answers, so judges must capture subtle domain-specific preferences. Existing rubric-based approaches often generate criteria online for each query, which increases inference cost and can yield rigid or misaligned guidance. The paper aims to provide a more efficient and reusable form of reward guidance.

Method: The paper proposes Eval-Skill, an exploration-guided framework that evolves reusable evaluation skills from only 100 cases per domain in two progressive stages: workflow generation followed by principle generation, with exploration and selection interleaved across both stages. The resulting skill is injected directly into the judge context at inference time.

Result: Across multiple reward-modeling benchmarks, Eval-Skill consistently improves a range of judge backbones. On RewardBench 2, it improves over vanilla judging by +13.44% for Qwen3-8B and +18.51% for DeepSeek-V4-Flash. Additional analyses suggest favorable scaling during evolution and good generalizability and transferability.

Conclusion: Eval-Skill demonstrates that compact, reusable domain-level evaluation skills can substantially improve open-ended reward modeling judges without per-query rubric generation or parameter training, offering an efficient and transferable paradigm for LLM-based evaluation.

Abstract: Open-ended reward modeling requires judges that can follow subtle, domain-specific preferences when verifiable answers are unavailable. Existing rubric-based methods often address this by generating criteria online for each query, but the extra generation step can add inference overhead and produce rigid or misaligned guidance. We introduce Eval-Skill, an exploration-guided method that synthesizes reusable evaluation skills for reward modeling and reframes reward guidance as context evolution rather than parameter training or per-query rubric generation. Using only 100 cases per domain for skill evolution, Eval-Skill synthesizes reusable domain-level evaluation skills through two progressive stages, workflow generation followed by principle generation, with exploration and selection interleaved across both stages. Once generated, a skill is directly injected into the judge context. Across multiple RM benchmarks, Eval-Skill consistently improves diverse judge backbones; on RewardBench 2, it yields significant gains over vanilla judging for each main backbone (+13.44% for Qwen3-8B, and 18.51% for DeepSeek-V4-Flash). Further analyses of evolution-time scaling, generalizability, and transferability show that compact evaluation skills offer an efficient new paradigm for LLM-based evaluation. Code is available at https://github.com/xing-stellus-yue/Eval-Skill.

</details>


### [131] [TRACE: Trajectory Reasoning through Adaptive Cross-Step Evidence Aggregation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07054)
*Vijitha Mittapalli,Shreyaa Jayant Dani,Satya Srujana Pilli,Snigdha Ansu,Mohammadreza Teymoorianfard,Franck Dernoncourt,Hongjie Chen,Yu Wang,Ryan A. Rossi,Nesreen K. Ahmed*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: TRACE improves long-horizon monitoring of LLM agents by iteratively triaging, inspecting, and judging trajectories while preserving cross-step evidence; it achieves strong detection performance on SHADE-Arena, particularly for long-range sabotage patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard trajectory monitoring struggles to detect sabotage when malicious objectives are spread across many individually benign actions. Existing methods either score the whole trajectory in one pass or split it into isolated windows, making long-range evidence linking difficult.

Method: The paper proposes TRACE, which uses a TIJ (Triage-Inspect-Judge) loop: it first identifies high-signal trajectory regions, then performs targeted inspection while carrying forward accumulated evidence across reasoning steps, and finally produces a trajectory-level judgment.

Result: On ten SHADE-Arena task domains, TRACE outperforms state-of-the-art baselines with an aggregate F1 of 0.713 and recall of 0.844, showing the largest improvements on tasks that require connecting temporally distant evidence.

Conclusion: TRACE is an effective long-horizon monitoring framework for detecting hidden malicious intent in autonomous LLM agent trajectories, especially when evidence must be linked across distant actions.

Abstract: Autonomous LLM agents can pursue hidden malicious objectives through sequences of individually benign actions, making sabotage difficult to detect using standard trajectory-level monitoring. Existing approaches either evaluate complete trajectories in a single pass or partition them into independently scored windows, limiting their ability to connect evidence across temporally distant actions. We propose TRACE, a monitoring framework for long-horizon LLM agent trajectories. TRACE operates through a TIJ (Triage-Inspect-Judge) loop that identifies high-signal regions, performs targeted inspection while maintaining accumulated evidence across reasoning steps, and synthesizes a trajectory-level verdict. We evaluate TRACE on ten task domains from SHADE-Arena against state-of-the-art baselines. TRACE achieves an aggregate F1 of 0.713 and recall of 0.844, with the largest gains on tasks requiring long-range evidence linking.

</details>


### [132] [Modeling semantic association in self-paced reading with language model embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07066)
*Sara Møller Østergaard,Kenneth Enevoldsen,Afra Alishahi,Bruno Nicenboim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper compares 10 ways to measure semantic association with LM embeddings in Dutch EEG and reading data and finds that the measurement choice matters a lot; sentence embeddings work best and capture effects beyond word predictability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although semantic association is known to matter for reading comprehension beyond word predictability, prior embedding-based operationalizations are inconsistent, so the paper evaluates how different implementation choices affect results.

Method: The study computes semantic association in ten implementations that vary language-model embedding type and context length on a Dutch natural-text corpus with joint EEG and self-paced reading, then tests effects on N400 and reading times using Bayesian hierarchical models and Bayes factors.

Result: Different embedding models yield different estimated effects of semantic association on both neural and behavioral measures. Only sentence-embedding-based implementations show reliable semantic-association effects beyond predictability on both N400 and self-paced reading times.

Conclusion: Methodological choices strongly influence embedding-based estimates of semantic association; sentence-embedding approaches most reliably capture semantic association beyond word predictability in both EEG N400 and self-paced reading.

Abstract: Semantic association between a word and its context has been identified as an important component of reading comprehension, even when word predictability is accounted for. Recent research has highlighted the potential of language model ( LM) embeddings to quantify semantic association. Yet, embedding-based semantic association have been operationalized in a myriad of ways. In this study, we use embeddings from LMs to estimate semantic association on a corpus of joint electroencephalography (EEG) and self-paced reading of natural, Dutch texts. Semantic association is calculated in ten different implementations that vary the embedding model and context lengths. The effects of semantic association across the different implementations on the N400 and self-paced reading times are examined using Bayesian hierarchical models and Bayes factor. The results show that the choice of embedding model can alter the estimated effect of semantic association on both the N400 and self-paced reading times. Furthermore, the results demonstrate a promising potential of sentence embeddings for capturing semantic association, as only implementations relying on sentence embeddings indicate reliable results of semantic association beyond word predictability on both neural and behavioral measures. Together, these findings highlight the importance of methodological choices in quantifying semantic association.

</details>


### [133] [mmPISA-bench: Do LLMs Reason Equally Well Across 43 Languages?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07069)
*Yerzhan Sapenov,Jaromir Savelka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: mmPISA-bench is a 43-language reasoning benchmark based on PISA. Two proprietary LLMs perform well across languages and roughly match human accuracy; machine-translated test items work nearly as well as official translations, though some languages are costlier and less accurate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multilingual reasoning evaluation resources are limited, expensive to build, and often lack broad language coverage with high-quality parallel data. The authors aim to provide a compact, high-quality benchmark to measure cross-lingual reasoning performance and to test whether machine translation is adequate for multilingual evaluation.

Method: The paper constructs mmPISA-bench from OECD PISA by selecting 25 reasoning-heavy multiple-choice questions, providing official human translations in 43 languages and machine-translated versions, and evaluating two mainstream proprietary LLMs across languages, reasoning effort levels, and translation types. It also analyzes token usage and inference cost by language.

Result: The benchmark contains 2,150 total data points (25 questions x 43 languages x 2 translation types). Experiments show effective reasoning across all evaluated languages, human-comparable accuracy overall, little to no accuracy loss from machine-translated questions, and notable cross-language differences in both accuracy and token-related inference cost.

Conclusion: Modern proprietary LLMs show strong multilingual reasoning on mmPISA-bench, often reaching human-comparable accuracy. High-quality machine-translated questions do not reduce accuracy versus official human translations, but performance and inference cost vary by language, with some languages being both more expensive and less accurate.

Abstract: We introduce mmPISA-bench, a compact high-quality multilingual reasoning benchmark derived from the OECD Programme for International Student Assessment (PISA). The benchmark consists of 25 multiple-choice questions that require reasoning in order to be answered correctly. Each question is provided in official human translations to 43 languages and complemented with machine-translated counterparts (i.e., 2,150 data points in total). We evaluate two mainstream proprietary LLMs across languages, reasoning effort levels, and translation types in terms of their ability to answer the questions correctly. Our results show that modern LLMs can reason effectively across all evaluated languages, achieve accuracy comparable to human test-takers, with some performance variations across covered languages. We further find that machine-translated questions do not degrade accuracy relative to official human translations which suggests that high-quality machine translation (synthetic data) might often be adequate for large-scale multilingual reasoning evaluations where official translations are not available. Finally, we analyze token usage and related inference cost and find that LLMs usage in some languages is simultaneously more expensive and less accurate.

</details>


### [134] [SigmaScale: LLM Compression with SVD-based Low-Rank Decomposition and Learned Scaling Matrices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07098)
*Ernests Lavrinovics,Marco Letizia,Roy Janco,Shai Segal,Johannes Bjerva,Maurizio Pierini*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SigmaScale learns activation-aware diagonal scaling before truncated SVD to make LLM weight matrices easier to compress, lowering effective rank and matching strong SVD-based baselines on major benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Analytical scaling strategies for SVD-based LLM compression may be too rigid to match the structure of individual weight matrices. The paper aims to learn more flexible scaling transformations that better reduce effective intrinsic rank and preserve model performance under low-rank compression.

Method: The method learns two sets of vectors that parameterize diagonal row and column scaling matrices for each weight matrix. These scaling transformations are optimized with an activation-aware compression loss, then truncated SVD is applied to the transformed weights for compression.

Result: On Llama 3.1 8B Instruct and Qwen3-8B, SigmaScale achieves performance competitive with closely related state-of-the-art SVD-based compression methods on perplexity and zero-shot benchmarks. The learned scaling also reduces effective-rank entropy, and this reduction is strongly correlated with lower compression loss.

Conclusion: SigmaScale demonstrates that learning activation-aware diagonal row and column scaling matrices before truncated SVD can improve low-rank LLM compression. It is a competitive and practical option for reducing inference cost, especially on some downstream tasks.

Abstract: We present SigmaScale, a method for learning auxiliary scaling matrices $S$ to aid truncated Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) based Large Language Model (LLM) compression. Instead of deriving scaling matrices analytically, SigmaScale optimizes two sets of vectors that define diagonal row and column scaling transformations under an activation-aware compression loss. We show that learned scaling lowers the effective intrinsic rank of weight matrices, as reflected by reductions in effective-rank entropy, and that this reduction is strongly correlated with compression loss. Experiments on Llama 3.1 8B Instruct and Qwen3-8B show that SigmaScale is competitive with closely related state-of-the-art SVD-based compression methods across perplexity and zero-shot benchmarks. By using learned activation-aware transformations, SigmaScale explores a more flexible route to low-rank LLM compression by adapting to the structure of individual model weights. The advantage observed in specific tasks makes our approach a valid option for applications requiring a reduced LLM-inference computing cost.

</details>


### [135] [Style or Content? Evaluating Style Classifiers with Controlled Content Overlap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07103)
*Zhuo Liu,Haozheng Du,Xiangxiang Xu,Hangfeng He*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Method: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Result: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Conclusion: This content has not passed the compliance test and has been hidden.

Abstract: Style classifiers can use content cues that correlate with style labels in naturally collected data, yet we lack a systematic way to measure this reliance. We study this problem with a controlled content overlap setup built on parallel Bible translations. Specifically, we define the overlap parameter $α$ as the normalized residual of mutual information between content identity and style label, so that it measures how much content is shared across style classes: from no shared content ($α=0$) to fully shared content ($α=1$). Cross-overlap evaluation of RoBERTa-based classifiers shows that low-overlap models degrade when content cues are removed, while high-overlap models transfer more robustly. A cross-style content retrieval probe further shows that content becomes less recoverable as $α$ increases, with training dynamics showing this removal occurs gradually. Together, these results suggest that controlled overlap provides a simple diagnostic for separating style learning from content shortcuts.

</details>


### [136] [Learning Perspectivist Social Meaning via Demographic-Conditioned Fusion Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07123)
*Amanda Cercas Curry,Lucio La Cava,Luca Maria Aiello,Gianmarco De Francisci Morales*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 论文研究如何让NLP尊重不同群体对语言社会意义的不同理解，并证明把人口统计信息与文本一起建模，能比只看文本更准确地预测这些差异。 / The paper shows that NLP can better model differing group interpretations of social meaning by combining demographic information with text, outperforming text-only approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 传统NLP通常把社会意义压缩为单一标签，忽视不同标注者因背景、人口属性和意识形态而产生的解释差异，因此需要一种能显式建模多元视角的方法。 / Standard NLP typically collapses social meaning into one label, ignoring interpretation differences driven by annotator background, demographics, and ideology, motivating an explicit perspectivist modeling approach.

Method: 作者基于2.8万条人工标注数据，将社会维度建模为一个随群体视角变化的谱系；比较了zero-shot、few-shot和fine-tuned等多种范式，并提出融合文本表示与人口统计表示的fusion embeddings。 / Using a dataset of 28k human annotations, the paper models social dimensions as a spectrum that varies by group perspective; it benchmarks zero-shot, few-shot, and fine-tuned paradigms, and proposes fusion embeddings that combine textual and demographic representations.

Result: 融合模型在所有融合策略上都稳定且统计显著地优于纯文本基线，相对macro PR-AUC提升5.9%到6.5%；shuffle消融表明人口统计特征包含真实预测信号，而非仅是伪相关。 / Across all fusion strategies, the fusion models consistently and significantly outperform text-only baselines with a 5.9% to 6.5% relative gain in macro PR-AUC; shuffle ablations show that demographic profiles provide genuine predictive signal rather than spurious correlation.

Conclusion: 建模语言社会意义时应采用perspectivist视角，而不是压缩为单一真值标签；融合文本与人口统计信息的模型能更好捕捉不同群体的解释差异，并显著优于纯文本基线。 / Language social meaning should be modeled perspectivally rather than as a single ground-truth label; models that fuse text with demographic information better capture group-specific interpretations and significantly outperform text-only baselines.

Abstract: Social meaning in language is inherently perspectival, varying across annotator backgrounds, demographics, and ideological positions. However, most NLP systems collapse this variation into a single ground-truth label, ignoring the diversity of interpretations. In this work, we model social dimensions along a perspectivist spectrum, capturing how interpretations vary across demographic groups on a dataset consisting of 28k human annotations. We benchmark multiple modeling paradigms, including zero-shot, few-shot, and fine-tuned approaches, and propose fusion embeddings that integrate textual and demographic representations. Our fusion models yield consistent and statistically significant improvements over text-only baselines across all fusion strategies (+5.9-6.5% relative macro PR-AUC), with shuffle ablations confirming that demographic profiles carry genuine predictive signal rather than spurious correlations.

</details>


### [137] [Explicit Evidence Grounding via Structured Inline Citation Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07130)
*Anar Yeginbergen,Amelie Wührl,Anna Rogers,Rodrigo Agerri*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FullCite 提出一种为问答生成结构化行内引用的方法，并比较了提示生成、约束解码和事后对齐三种策略。结果显示，大模型会找文档，但不擅长找精确证据句段，未来提升忠实引用的关键在于更好的证据跨度识别。 / FullCite proposes structured inline citations for attributed QA and compares prompting, constrained decoding, and posthoc alignment. The key finding is that LLMs can usually find the right documents but struggle to pinpoint exact evidence spans, making evidence span identification the critical next step.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 随着 AI 系统广泛应用，用户对事实性和忠实生成的要求提高，因此需要更可靠的引用机制来明确每个生成声明的来源与依据。现有方法多只做粗粒度引用，缺少将声明精确对齐到文档和证据片段的结构化行内引用。 / As AI systems are adopted more broadly, the need for factual and faithful generation increases, making reliable attribution essential. Prior work often provides only coarse-grained citation, lacking structured inline citations that precisely align each claim with both source documents and evidence spans.

Method: 论文提出 FullCite 框架，用于生成结构化行内引用，将每个声明同时链接到源文档及其支持证据。框架探索了三种策略：基于提示的生成、基于引用语法的约束解码，以及事后跨度对齐；并在 ASQA、BioASQ、ExpertQA 三个问答基准上，从文档级正确性、证据片段识别、声明-引用忠实性三个维度进行评估。 / The paper introduces FullCite, a framework for generating structured inline citations that connect each claim to both a source document and supporting evidence. It studies three strategies: prompt-based generation, constrained decoding with a citation grammar, and posthoc span alignment; evaluation is conducted on ASQA, BioASQ, and ExpertQA across document-level correctness, evidence span identification, and claim-citation faithfulness.

Result: 实验表明，模型通常能够找到相关文档，但在这些文档内部识别精确支持片段时表现较差。这说明引用质量的主要瓶颈不在文档检索，而在细粒度证据定位。 / The experiments show that models are generally effective at finding relevant documents, but they struggle to identify precise supporting spans within those documents. This indicates that the main bottleneck in citation quality lies less in document retrieval and more in fine-grained evidence localization.

Conclusion: 现有大语言模型在文档级引用匹配上表现尚可，但在定位精确证据片段方面明显不足；要实现真正忠实、可归因的问答生成，后续研究需要更重视细粒度证据跨度识别。 / Current LLMs perform reasonably well at document-level citation selection but remain weak at locating precise supporting evidence spans; faithful attributed QA will require stronger emphasis on fine-grained evidence span identification.

Abstract: As AI systems become more widely adopted, the demand for factual and faithful generation grows. Properly attributing information through citations becomes, therefore, crucial. This work introduces FullCite, a framework that, in contrast to most previous works, generates structured inline citations linking each claim to both its source document and supporting evidence. FullCite proposes three strategies to inline citation generation: prompt-based generation, constrained decoding over a citation grammar, and posthoc span alignment. Using three question answering benchmarks, namely, ASQA, BioASQ, and ExpertQA, we assess citation quality and faithfulness along three dimensions: document-level correctness, evidence span identification, and claim-citation faithfulness. Our evaluation shows that while LLMs are generally effective at identifying relevant documents, they struggle to identify the precise supporting spans within them. This gap suggests that achieving faithful attributed QA will require research to place greater emphasis on precise evidence span identification.

</details>


### [138] [UrduMMLU: A Massive Multitask Benchmark for Urdu Language Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07167)
*Ahmer Tabassum,Sarfraz Ahmad,Hasan Iqbal,Owais Aijaz,Momina Ahsan,Preslav Nakov*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a large native Urdu benchmark and shows that even strong LLMs still struggle with authentic Urdu and region-specific knowledge, especially outside STEM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multilingual evaluation is often translation-based and fails to reflect the target language and educational context. Urdu lacks a large native-source MMLU-style benchmark despite its broad speaker base.

Method: The paper builds UrduMMLU from native Urdu MCQ banks and public exam PDFs, applies dual human annotation with strict consensus filtering for exam-derived items, and evaluates 30 LLMs in zero-shot under both English and Urdu prompts plus four open-source LLMs in multiple few-shot settings.

Result: UrduMMLU contains 26,431 Urdu multiple-choice questions across 26 subjects and five domains. Gemini-3.5-Flash achieves the top accuracy at 90.20% and 90.34%, no other model exceeds 85%, the best open-source model lags by 7.79 and 8.92 points, and many models drop 25–40 points on Urdu-centered humanities relative to STEM.

Conclusion: UrduMMLU reveals that current LLMs have uneven Urdu knowledge, with especially weak performance on regionally grounded and Urdu-centered humanities content; few-shot prompting offers only limited improvement.

Abstract: Meaningful multilingual evaluation must test models in the target language and educational context. Urdu, spoken by more than 230 million people, lacks a broad MMLU-style benchmark built from native educational sources. We introduce UrduMMLU, a benchmark of 26,431 Urdu MCQs across 26 subjects and five domains, collected from native Urdu MCQ banks and public examination PDFs. Unlike translation-based resources, UrduMMLU covers both standard academic subjects and Urdu- and region-specific content. We label the exam-derived portion through dual human annotation with strict consensus filtering. We evaluate 30 LLMs under English and Urdu prompts, yielding 60 zero-shot evaluations, and further evaluate four open-source LLMs under multiple few-shot settings across both prompt languages. Gemini-3.5-Flash performs best, reaching 90.20% and 90.34% accuracy, while no other model exceeds 85%. The strongest open-source model trails by 7.79 and 8.92 points, and many models lose 25 to 40 points on Urdu-centered Humanities subjects compared with STEM. Few-shot prompting yields only modest gains. UrduMMLU shows that Urdu knowledge remains uneven in current LLMs, especially for regionally grounded content.

</details>


### [139] [Geometry of Semantic Space: Comparative Study of Discrete and Continuous Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07183)
*Gabriel Bounias,Sabine Ploux*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper compares transformer embeddings with co-occurrence graphs for semantic geometry and finds that they align locally but differ globally; graphs appear more interpretable and could help guide neural models toward more stable semantic representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by the observation that strong-performing transformer embeddings often induce semantic geometries with unsatisfactory distributions, while graph-based representations may offer clearer and more human-readable semantic organization.

Method: The paper implements a comparative methodology that analyzes semantic geometry from two perspectives: structural analysis of lexical co-occurrence graphs and topological analysis of induced embedding spaces from supervised models such as CamemBERT.

Result: On the studied corpus, the comparison shows similar local topology between the two approaches but markedly different overall structure and topology, highlighting their complementarity rather than simple equivalence.

Conclusion: Graph-based semantic representations and supervised transformer embeddings provide complementary views of meaning: they can share similar local topology while differing strongly in global structure, suggesting graph structures may help improve interpretability and stability in neural semantic modeling.

Abstract: This work examines the semantic geometry underlying NLP models. We compare supervised vector embeddings, such as CamemBERT, with lexical co-occurrence graphs that encode semantic relations more directly. While transformer-based embeddings achieve strong performance, their induced geometries often display unsatisfactory distributions. In contrast, graph-based models reveal a clearer and more human-readable organization of meaning. We have implemented a methodology that allows us to perform a comparative analysis either based on the structure of the graphs or based on the topology of the embeddings induced by these two approaches. The results of the comparison -- applied to the French "Great National Debate" corpus a collection of citizen contributions to the public debate -- show a similar local topology but a very different overall structure and topology. Theses findings suggest complementary perspectives between deep supervised models and graph-based models, considering a new pathway to guide neural architectures toward more stable and interpretable convergence with graphs structures.

</details>


### [140] [From Correctness to Utility: Gain-Based Prefix Evaluation for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07190)
*Yuhang Zhou,Yixin Cao,Guangnan Ye*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper replaces step-level correctness scoring with an outcome-based measure called prefix gain, then trains PUM to rank reasoning prefixes by how much they improve final solve rate; this yields stronger supervision for math reasoning search and RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing process reward models mainly judge prefixes by local correctness of intermediate steps, but local correctness is only an indirect proxy for the real goal: whether a prefix helps the model eventually solve the problem. The work aims to learn a utility signal aligned with final task success.

Method: The paper defines prefix gain as the solve-rate improvement induced when a lightweight student model group is conditioned on a given prefix. Using this outcome-grounded signal, the authors train a Prefix Utility Model (PUM) with a simple pairwise ranking objective so it can score both full reasoning trajectories and partial prefixes.

Result: Across Best-of-N selection, beam search, and reinforcement learning for math reasoning, PUM consistently improves performance and shows larger advantages when candidate sets are bigger, search budgets are larger, or rule-based rewards are sparse. The authors also release data, models, and code.

Conclusion: Prefix Utility Model (PUM) provides a more direct and effective supervision signal than local step-correctness based process reward models by estimating whether a reasoning prefix improves downstream solve probability. It works well for trajectory selection, search, and RL in mathematical reasoning, especially under large candidate pools, higher search budgets, or sparse rewards.

Abstract: Reasoning prefixes shape the future trajectory of LLM problem solving, yet existing process reward models usually evaluate them through local step correctness. We argue that correctness is a useful but indirect proxy for the effect we ultimately care about: whether a prefix increases the probability of successful completion. We define this effect as prefix gain, the solve-rate improvement induced by conditioning lightweight student model group on a prefix, and use it to train a Prefix Utility Model (PUM) with a simple pairwise ranking objective. PUM learns outcome-grounded prefix utility and can score both complete trajectories and partial reasoning prefixes. Across Best-of-$N$ selection, beam search, and reinforcement learning on mathematical reasoning, PUM provides a strong prefix-level supervision signal, especially when candidate pools are large, search budgets increase, or rule-based rewards are sparse. We release all data, models, and code at https://zhiqix.github.io/pum-project-page.

</details>


### [141] [Adversarial Creation and Detection of AI-Generated Social Bot Content](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07219)
*Mykola Trokhymovych,Ricardo Baeza-Yates,Alessandro Flammini,Diego Saez-Trumper,Filippo Menczer*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Create adversarial, realistic human-vs-AI social media text pairs, train on them, and AI-text detection becomes much more robust in the wild.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AI-generated content detectors often fail in real-world settings because they lack reliable ground-truth data and do not capture how malicious actors actually use large language models for social-bot impersonation.

Method: The paper proposes an adversarial methodology that simulates malicious actors impersonating real social media users, then uses it to build a multilingual, cross-platform dataset of paired human-written and AI-generated messages for detector training.

Result: The resulting detector trained on the adversarial dataset achieves accurate AI-text detection and significantly surpasses existing content-based bot detection approaches on real-world, out-of-distribution evaluations.

Conclusion: Training detectors on adversarially generated, paired human/AI social-media messages substantially improves real-world detection of AI-generated text and outperforms prior content-based bot detection models on out-of-distribution data.

Abstract: The convergence of large language models and social bots allows malicious actors to manipulate the information ecosystem by generating human-like content at scale. Existing models for detecting AI-generated content often fail in the wild, primarily due to the lack of ground-truth data. We address this gap through an adversarial methodology that models the impersonation of real social media users by malicious actors. Using this methodology, we curate a multilingual, cross-platform dataset of paired human and AI-generated messages. Training on such adversarial data yields accurate detection of AI-generated text. Our approach significantly outperforms existing models for content-based bot detection in real-world, out-of-distribution data.

</details>


### [142] [When Large Language Models Fail in Healthcare: Evaluating Sensitivity to Prompt Variations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07237)
*Mahdi Alkaeed*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: 这篇论文说明：医疗LLM即使经过领域化训练，也会因轻微改写而改变诊断或生成危险建议；它们在真实临床部署前需要更强的鲁棒性评测与安全防护。 / The paper shows that even domain-adapted medical LLMs can change diagnoses or produce unsafe advice after minor prompt changes, so stronger robustness evaluation and safety safeguards are required before real clinical deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 尽管LLM已被广泛用于临床问答、诊断支持和报告摘要，但它们对细微词汇和句法变化高度敏感，这在安全关键的医疗场景中可能带来严重风险，因此需要系统评估其鲁棒性。 / Although LLMs are increasingly used for clinical QA, diagnostic support, and report summarization, their sensitivity to subtle lexical and syntactic changes can create serious risks in safety-critical healthcare settings, motivating a systematic robustness evaluation.

Method: 论文基于MedMCQA基准，对通用模型（如GPT-3.5、Llama3）和医学专用模型（如ClinicalBERT、BioLlama3、BioBERT）进行系统性敏感性分析；将扰动分为自然扰动和对抗扰动，并评估其对一致性、准确性与临床推理可靠性的影响。 / The paper performs a systematic sensitivity analysis on general-purpose models (e.g., GPT-3.5, Llama3) and medical-specific models (e.g., ClinicalBERT, BioLlama3, BioBERT) using the MedMCQA benchmark, categorizing perturbations into natural and adversarial types and measuring their effects on consistency, accuracy, and reliability in clinical reasoning.

Result: 研究发现：模型对简单词汇替换或释义通常较稳健，但在句法重排或误导性上下文下性能明显退化；这种脆弱性同时存在于通用和医学专用模型中。更重要的是，对抗性提示可能诱发临床危险输出，例如错误剂量建议或遗漏关键发现。 / The study finds that models are generally more resilient to simple lexical substitutions or paraphrases, but often fail under syntactic reordering or misleading contextual cues; this fragility appears in both general-purpose and medical-specific LLMs. More critically, adversarial prompts can trigger clinically dangerous outputs such as incorrect dosage recommendations or omission of critical findings.

Conclusion: 医疗与通用大语言模型在临床任务中对提示扰动表现出显著脆弱性，医学领域专用模型并不天然安全；在医疗这类高风险场景中，当前这类不稳定性使其难以被可靠信任。 / Both medical-specific and general-purpose LLMs show substantial fragility to prompt perturbations in clinical tasks, and medical specialization does not make them inherently safe; this level of instability makes them hard to trust in high-stakes healthcare settings.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used in healthcare for tasks such as clinical question answering, diagnosis support, and report summarization. Despite their promise, these models remain highly sensitive to subtle prompt perturbations, both lexical and syntactic, posing serious risks in safety-critical clinical applications. In this study, we conduct a systematic sensitivity analysis to evaluate the robustness of both general-purpose (e.g., GPT-3.5, Llama3) and medical-specific LLMs (e.g., ClinicalBERT, BioLlama3, BioBERT) using the MedMCQA benchmark. We categorize perturbations into natural and adversarial types and examine their effect on model consistency, accuracy, and reliability in clinical reasoning tasks. Our findings reveal that medical LLMs are not intrinsically safe. Even minor variations in phrasing can alter clinical advice, and targeted adversarial prompts can provoke harmful outputs. In high-stakes settings like healthcare, such unpredictability is unacceptable-models that change diagnoses due to reworded inputs or hallucinate medications when slightly rephrased cannot be reliably trusted by clinicians. While models tend to show resilience to simple lexical substitutions or paraphrasing, they often break down under syntactic reordering or misleading contextual cues. This fragility is evident across both general-purpose and domain-specific LLMs. Notably, adversarial manipulations can lead to clinically dangerous outputs, such as recommending incorrect dosages or omitting critical findings.

</details>


### [143] [KIT's Submission to Cross-Lingual Voice Cloning in IWSLT 2026](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07240)
*Seymanur Akti,Alexander Waibel*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A multilingual TTS system for cross-lingual voice cloning is improved mainly by language tag prompting, with extra gains from RL adaptation and lexical matching for specialized terms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-lingual voice cloning must preserve speaker identity while generating intelligible, natural target-language speech, but it is challenged by accent leakage and domain-specific vocabulary; this is especially important for speech translation and the IWSLT 2026 track.

Method: The paper builds on the multilingual TTS model FishAudio-S2-Pro, adds language tag prompting for better language control, applies reinforcement learning fine-tuning for task adaptation, and introduces a reference-conditioned lexical matching method to improve term pronunciation.

Result: Experiments show that language prompting produces the biggest gains overall, RL improves intelligibility, and lexical matching gives consistent improvements on subsets with lexical overlap.

Conclusion: Language tag prompting delivers the largest improvement for cross-lingual voice cloning, RL fine-tuning further improves intelligibility, and reference-conditioned lexical matching consistently helps pronunciation of domain-specific terms when lexical overlap exists.

Abstract: Cross-lingual voice cloning aims to generate speech in a target language while preserving speaker identity from a source-language reference. This task is central to speech translation and is the focus of the IWSLT 2026 Cross-Lingual Voice Cloning track. A key challenge is maintaining intelligibility and naturalness in the presence of accent variation and domain-specific vocabulary. We build on a multilingual text-to-speech model, FishAudio-S2-Pro, and introduce language tag prompting to improve language control and reduce accent leakage. We further apply reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning for task adaptation and observe improvements in intelligibility. Finally, we propose a reference-conditioned lexical matching method that improves pronunciation of domain-specific terms when lexical overlap is present. Results show that language prompting provides the largest gains, while lexical matching yields consistent improvements on matched subsets.

</details>


### [144] [Phun-Bench: Evaluating LLMs on Phonological Understanding in Chinese](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07300)
*Xing Yue,Yongliang Shen,Weiming Lu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Phun-Bench is a new Chinese benchmark for testing whether LLMs truly understand sound patterns. It finds that LLMs remember pronunciations better than they reason with them, revealing a key weakness in phonological competence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior LLM evaluation emphasizes semantics and spelling while neglecting sound. Existing phonology benchmarks are either too easy due to memorization or mixed with other abilities, so they cannot accurately measure true phonological understanding.

Method: The paper introduces Phun-Bench, a dedicated Chinese benchmark for evaluating phonological understanding across three dimensions: Homophony, Rhyme, and Phonetic Similarity, using diverse tasks and settings to isolate genuine phonological ability from memorization and confounded skills.

Result: Experiments show that current LLMs perform well at recalling standard pronunciations, but generally struggle when they must apply phonological knowledge in more flexible, intuitive, human-like ways. The authors also derive an explanatory hypothesis about how LLM phonological 'perception' may work.

Conclusion: LLMs can often recall Chinese pronunciations correctly, but they do not yet use phonological knowledge flexibly or human-likely across homophony, rhyme, and phonetic similarity tasks; phonological understanding remains an underexplored limitation and research direction.

Abstract: Language is a vehicle for thought, intricately tied to sounds, symbols, and meaning. However, most large language model (LLM) research focuses on meaning (semantics) and symbols (spelling) while largely overlooking sounds. Existing benchmarks on LLMs' phonological abilities are either solvable through rote memorization or intertwined with other abilities, making them inadequate to measure LLMs' genuine ability in phonological understanding. Here, we present Phun-Bench, a purpose-built Chinese benchmark with diverse tasks and settings across three dimensions (Homophony, Rhyme, and Phonetic Similarity), designed to systematically evaluate LLMs' phonological understanding. Our results show that while LLMs excel at recalling correct pronunciations, they generally struggle to leverage phonological knowledge in the flexible and intuitive way that human speakers do. Moreover, through detailed analyses, we propose a hypothesis regarding the underlying mechanism of LLMs' phonological understanding and "perception", highlighting an underexplored frontier for future research.

</details>


### [145] [SV-Detect: AI-generated Text Detection with Steering Vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.07313)
